# Aert van der Neer: Study Notes

**Site:** Aert (Aernout, Artus) van der Neer (c. 1603/04, Gorinchem or Amsterdam - 9 November 1677, Amsterdam), Dutch Golden Age landscape painter; the great specialist in moonlight of seventeenth-century Dutch painting, who made two genres his own -- moonlit river landscapes and winter scenes -- and almost nothing else. Steward to the lords of Arkel turned late-starting painter, innkeeper and wine merchant from 1659, bankrupt 12 December 1662, dead in poverty in Amsterdam with his pictures valued at five guilders or less in the bankruptcy inventory (the famous "five shillings apiece" is the Britannica tradition -- see leg 1 F8). Father of Eglon Hendrik van der Neer (1635/36-1703) and of the landscape painter Johannes (Jan) van der Neer (1637/8-1665). Birthplace is disputed: Gorinchem per the 2008 archival research of Rene van Dijk, Amsterdam per the NGA and the Stadel; birth year c. 1603/04, never a single certain year. Richard Green calls him "unrivalled as a painter of moonlight in Dutch art"; the V&A calls coloured light the essential characteristic of his style.

**Doorway:** Hop from the Eglon van der Neer study: the father whose moonlight landscapes Eglon was first taught on -- and then left behind for the elegant interior, only to return to landscape at the end. The chain is bench, then departure, then return: Aert taught Eglon first, before Jacob van Loo made him a figure painter; Eglon then spent his career painting fashionable interiors and small enamel-finished figure groups, and in his last Brussels and Dusseldorf years set his history scenes outdoors in landscape again, "following the example of Adam Elsheimer" and imitating his father's landscapes. Three generations on one bench, since Werff then learned the same drapery finish from Eglon. The doorway is the father whose night pictures the son was taught on, left, and came back to -- and this study is the nocturne itself as a system, the mechanism Eglon's study closes on and the landscape line opens with.

## The life

Aert van der Neer was born around 1603 or 1604 -- c. 1603 in Wikipedia, 1603/04 in the NGA, the Stadel, the V&A and Academia Aesthetics, 1604 in Richard Green, "December 1603 or early 1604" in two secondary sites -- and died in Amsterdam on 9 November 1677, aged about seventy-three. Where he was born is disputed, and the dispute is live: Gorinchem, per the 2008 research of Rene van Dijk of the Gorinchem Regional Archive (Wikipedia, Lempertz, Richard Green, V&A, Academia Aesthetics), against Amsterdam, probably 1603/04, per the NGA (Wheelock, updated 2018) and the Stadel. The 2008 archival research is the newer evidence and Wikipedia and Lempertz treat it as settled; the NGA still says "probably born in Amsterdam". This study gives Gorinchem with the Amsterdam tradition footnoted, and presents neither as certain (leg 1 F1). His parents were Egerom (Egrom) Aertsz. van der Neer and Aeltje (Aeltge) Jansdr.; no source read gives their occupations or dates. A document dated 12 May 1642 gives his age as "roughly thirty-four", which taken literally points to a birth c. 1608, four to five years later than every other dating; no source read addresses the discrepancy (leg 1 F3).

He spent his youth as a steward (majoor) to the lords of Arkel, which Houbraken says accounts for the absence of any pictures dating from his early years; the NGA, more precisely, has him working, likely as a steward, for Wilhelmina van Arkel, the widow of the city's highest town official, near Gorinchem. By the early 1630s he had moved to Amsterdam -- "by 1628" (Richard Green), "by 1630" (National Gallery), "in about 1632" (V&A, Academia) -- and remained there for the rest of his life. In 1629 he was living at Herenmarkt near the Brouwersgracht. On 16 March 1629 he married Lysbeth Goverts (Lijsbeth Govaerts; spellings vary) in Amsterdam's Nieuwe Kerk; on the marriage licence he is identified as a painter, suggesting he had already completed his training -- which cuts against Houbraken's "amateur painter" framing. His wife was the sister of the Amsterdam painters Rafael Govertsz Camphuysen (1597/98-1657) and Jochem (Joachim) Govertsz Camphuysen (1601/02-1659).

The couple had six named children -- Grietje (1629), Eglon (~1635), Cornelia (1642), Elisabeth (1645), Pieter (1648), Alida (1650), five baptised in the Nieuwe Kerk -- or seven, per the Stadel. The seventh is presumably Johannes (Jan) van der Neer (1637/8-1665), named by Richard Green as a son who "followed his father as a landscape painter" and by Bonhams as the son with whom Aert kept the tavern, but absent from Wikipedia's six names (leg 1 F4). Eglon's own birth year appears in Aert-side sources as 1634 (NGA, Richard Green), against ~1635 (Wikipedia) and 1635/36 (Schavemaker, per #550); this study uses 1635/36 for Eglon and footnotes 1634 (leg 1 F5).

Nothing is known of his training. Bonhams says Rafael Camphuysen was "probably" his master; Lempertz says it was probably the company of his brothers-in-law that inspired him to paint, and "just as likely that he received his first instruction in their studio"; the Stadel says only collaboration with Joachim is documented; Richard Green records that he worked in Amsterdam with Jochem Camphuysen in 1633; the NGA says it is not known when he left the Gorinchem area or where and with whom he trained, and that "it seems that he developed his style rather independently". The RKD, via Wikipedia and Google Arts and Culture, lists Jacob Gerritsz. Cuyp as his teacher -- a competing claim, unresolved in this run (leg 1 F11; leg 3 F6). He is called a painter in 1629, but his first known work dates from 1632: a genre scene, now in the Narodni Galerie, Prague, a type of subject to which he never returned. His first dated landscape is from 1633; in 1635 he painted his first commissioned landscape, of large dimension; further commissions followed around 1640, and only after 1643 did he find his personal style.

During the early years of his career he painted realistic tonal landscapes and winter scenes inspired by Rafael Camphuysen, Esaias van de Velde I (1587-1630) and Hendrick Avercamp (1585-1634), with the Flemish landscape tradition reaching him through the Camphuysen brothers and the Frankenthal school (Gillis d'Hondecoeter, Alexander Keirincx). The earliest pictures in which he coupled his interlaced monogram of A.V. and D.N. with a date are a winter landscape in the Rijksmuseum (dated 1639) and another in the Martins collection at Kiel (1642), "immature works both, of poor quality" in Britannica's verdict; the NGA dates his first dated winter landscape to 1642, and Thyssen says he had begun painting winter scenes by 1641.

1643 is the turn. His first real evening landscape was the painting of 1643 now in Schloss Friedenstein, Gotha; the NGA agrees the earliest known nocturne is dated 1643; Britannica praises the Winter Landscape (1643) and the Moonlight Scene (1644, once in the d'Arenberg collection, Brussels). From 1643 onwards he occupied himself intensively with the phenomena of illumination and light, and developed the characteristic moonlit landscape; the second half of the 1640s was the most productive period of his life. His first really remarkable paintings of this kind are the moonlit river landscape of circa 1646 from the Six Collection, now in the Rijksmuseum, and the townscape by moonlight in the Museum Bredius at The Hague. The classic nocturnes follow: Moonlit Landscape with a View of the New Amstel River and Castle Kostverloren, 1647, Getty (monogrammed AVDN 1647 on the tree stump); Moonlit Landscape with Bridge, probably 1648/1650, National Gallery of Art Washington; Moonlit landscape, signed and dated AV DN 1647, oil on oak panel 83.8 x 114.9 cm (Schulz lists it as dated 1645 -- see leg 1 F10); A River near a Town, by Moonlight, c. 1645, National Gallery NG239; A Village by a River in Moonlight, c. 1645, National Gallery NG2536. Around 1650 came large summer landscapes, and in the 1650s small, jewel-like paintings for collectors' cabinets.

In 1652 he witnessed the fire which consumed the old town hall of Amsterdam. Britannica says he made the accident the subject of two or three pictures, now in Berlin and Copenhagen; Sotheby's 2011 lot note corrects this -- "although van der Neer is not known to have created a documentary work himself, the dramatic blaze may well have inspired the depictions of fiery landscapes within his oeuvre". The Berlin Gemaldegalerie holds a moonlit landscape with a burning village (Schulz no. 1357); the Statens Museum for Kunst, Copenhagen, holds Fire in Amsterdam by Night, oil on canvas 58.8 x 71.7 cm. Use Sotheby's correction: he painted fires; he is not documented as painting THE fire (leg 1 F9).

Substantial evidence exists, per Britannica, that there was friendship between him and Aelbert Cuyp, and that at some period they laid their hands to the same canvases: Cuyp's fishermen with a dog and sportsman creeping up on ducks in a Van der Neer at the Staedel in Frankfurt; the Louvre landscape sunset in which Cuyp evidently painted the foreground and cows; the National Gallery London picture in which Cuyp signs his name on the pail of a milkmaid, whose figure and red skirt he painted near the edge of Van der Neer's landscape. Lempertz: Cuyp probably assisted Aert and enlivened his landscapes with figures. Wikipedia's verdict: "Cuyp was the leading genius. Van Der Neer got assistance from him; Cuyp expected none from Van Der Neer."

In 1659 he was identified as the innkeeper of the "Graeff van Hollant" in the Kalverstraat, and named, together with his son, as a "wyntapper". The tavern failed three years later, and on 12 December 1662 he declared bankruptcy; a document of 21 March 1663 tells us that three months later he was already debt-free, yet he spent the rest of his life in poverty. From about 1660 onwards he began to focus primarily on winter landscapes; Frozen River at Sunset, painted in or shortly after 1660, is described by Schulz as a masterpiece. He continued to paint, probably until the beginning of the 1670s; the V&A gives his latest work as 1665, Richard Green his activity into the early 1670s (leg 1 F12).

He died at Kerkstraat in abject poverty on 9 November 1677. The inventory of his possessions made at the bankruptcy indicates that most of his paintings were appraised at five guilders or less; Britannica's "about five shillings apiece" is the English retelling of the same poverty, not the document figure. His sons declined their inheritance in order to afford a proper burial for their father. His wife, Lysbeth, had predeceased him; he was buried, on a single secondary source, in the Nieuwe Zijds Kapel (leg 1 F14).

Afterlife: copies after his works were already in existence in 1664, in his own lifetime. The demand for his moonlit landscapes was great during his lifetime and again in the 18th and 19th centuries, particularly 1770-80 and 1815-1840 in central Europe and England under the influence of Romanticism, which led to a series of copies and forgeries often inscribed with fake monograms. Taste changed in Amsterdam in the later 1660s, and he ended his days in poverty. By the 19th century the Hermitage at Saint Petersburg held the choicest selection of his roughly one hundred and fifty pictures accessible to the public. His nine most expensive paintings sold at auction are winter scenes.

## M1. The coloured-light nocturne: moonlight and firelight as the only light sources

What it IS: the defining system. Wikipedia's lead: Van der Neer "specialized in small night scenes lit only by moonlight and fires, and snowy winter landscapes." Britannica: his skill is "rendering translucence, which allows objects even at distance to appear in the darkness with varieties of warm brown and steel greys." V&A: "He used a restricted palette of earthy colours to represent isolated figures involved in daily activities in expansive landscapes in which a wide sky is generally the source of light. *Coloured light* is thus typically considered the essential characteristic of Aert van der Neer's style." The two light sources are the moon (cool, silvery, distant) and fire (warm, orange-red, near: a burning village, a lit window, a bonfire on the bank). How it shows: the whole picture is a contest between two temperatures of light -- the moon's cold track on the water against the fire's warm bloom on the roofs. Nothing else is allowed to glow. The eye keeps moving between the two lamps. -- Wikipedia (123 lines, read); V&A Moonlight and River Scene (114 lines, read); Britannica via Wikipedia.

**Takeaway:** two lamps and nothing else. A piece built on this mechanism must allow exactly two light sources -- one cool and distant, one warm and near -- and let the eye travel between them, with everything else in warm dark.

## M2. The imprimatura as mid-tone: the ground does the glowing

What it IS: a technical procedure documented by the NGA's conservation entry on *Moonlit Landscape with Bridge* (46 lines, read): "He suggested the translucency of the clouds, for example, by allowing the reddish ocher imprimatura covering the walnut panel to remain visible through thinly applied bluish gray paint." The technical note adds: "The thick fawn-colored imprimatura is incorporated as a mid-tone in the foreground and sky," and "The wood grain is plainly visible through the smooth, extremely thin white ground." The sky glows from within because the warm ground is never fully covered. Academia Aesthetics (55 lines, read): "He worked in a narrow range of browns and silvery grays, scratching into wet paint and laying thin glazes so that the sky glows from within." How it shows: clouds are translucent, not opaque -- you see the warm ground through the blue-grey paint, so the sky reads as lit from behind. The moon's halo is a thinning of paint, not an addition of white. -- NGA Moonlit Landscape with Bridge article (read); Academia Aesthetics artist page (read).

**Takeaway:** the light is under the paint, not on it. A piece built on this mechanism must make the sky glow by leaving the warm ground visible through thin paint -- the halo is where the paint thins, not where white is added.

## M3. Scratch and stipple: highlights made by removal

What it IS: a mark-making procedure, again from the NGA technical entry: "In the foreground, he articulated a number of objects with light contours, sometimes highlighted with paint applied with a brush and sometimes by scratching the wet paint with an instrument to reveal the ground layer." "The paint was applied in thin layers. Brushwork is prominent in the sky, and stippling was employed in the foliage and lawn. Highlights were sometimes created by the application of light-colored paint, and sometimes by scratching into the dark paint to reveal the lighter imprimatura below." Princeton on *River Landscape in Moonlight* (76 lines, read): "The earth tones and thin paint application in the darks are complemented by the blue-gray colors and use of impasto dots and dashes in the sky and water." Sotheby's Frozen River condition report: "some sketchy lines of underdrawing are visible in the landscape under close inspection" and "A few pentimenti can be seen amidst the skating figures." How it shows: the brightest marks in the picture -- a glint on water, a reed edge, a star -- are scratches through dark paint to the pale ground beneath. Light is literally uncovered, not painted on. Close looking finds the tool-marks; distance hides them. -- NGA technical entry (read); Princeton object page (read); Sotheby's Frozen River condition report (read).

**Takeaway:** light is uncovered, not applied. A piece built on this mechanism must make its brightest marks by removing darkness -- scratches and stipple that reveal the pale ground -- so the highlights read as holes in the night rather than paint on it.

## M4. The river as perspective machine: low horizon, receding water

What it IS: the compositional formula, repeated across the oeuvre. Academia Aesthetics: "a river runs into the distance between dark banks of trees and houses, and the moon, often half-hidden by cloud, sets the edges of the clouds and the water alight." National Gallery (An Evening Landscape with a Horse and Cart): "he used the sinuous path of a river or inlet receding into the distance to emphasise the effect" of depth. Sotheby's Frozen River note: the broad frozen river stretching to the horizon, "the carefully arranged trees, backed by a row of houses and sheds, providing a rhythmic line of verticals along the left bank," the opposite bank a wall, "in the distance the roofs and towers of a town." The low horizon gives most of the panel to the sky (Academia on the Getty Moonlit Landscape: "The low horizon gives most of the panel to the sky"). How it shows: the river is a road the eye walks -- near bank dark and detailed, far bank dissolving into mist and cloud-bank. The verticals on one bank tick past like fence posts while the water pulls the eye to the vanishing point. -- Academia Aesthetics artist page and Getty Moonlit Landscape page (read); National Gallery artist page (read); Sotheby's Frozen River note (read).

**Takeaway:** the water is the road and the sky is the picture. A piece built on this mechanism must put the horizon low, give most of the frame to the sky, and let a receding river pull the eye to a vanishing point past a rhythm of bank verticals.

## M5. The composite place: an imaginary Holland assembled from real parts

What it IS: a working method, stated plainly by the NGA: "In conceiving this image, Van der Neer was more interested in creating the mood of nature than in recording an actual setting. The large houses to the left are similar to those found in Amsterdam... but the church adjacent to the dwellings beyond recall those found in smaller cities and towns... The setting, thus, is a composite of various aspects of city and country life that Van der Neer has brought into a harmonious whole." National Gallery NG2536: "It probably doesn't depict a 'real' place, but one composed by the artist so that he could highlight the reflections of light on the mirror-still water, and the carefully arranged silhouettes of the church, the windmill and the boats lying off the quayside." NGA biography: "these views, as well as his woodland and winter scenes, are, in fact, fanciful re-creations of the Dutch countryside." The Getty Kostverloren picture is the rare exception: an identifiable landmark, "a free rendering of one of the primary landmarks along the river" (Getty CONA, search result); Google Arts and Culture: "Van der Neer usually depicted imaginary places in his landscape paintings. In this rare example, however, he painted an identifiable landmark, the Castle Kostverloren. The evocative river setting, painted inside a studio, is not accurate." How it shows: the picture feels exactly like a real Dutch river at night and is not one -- Amsterdam houses, a small-town church, a rural castle ruin, all composited in the studio. The viewer trusts the place because the light is consistent, not because the geography is. -- NGA Moonlit Landscape with Bridge article (read); National Gallery NG2536 (read); NGA biography via docslib (read); Getty CONA record (search result).

**Takeaway:** the place is invented; the light is what is true. A piece built on this mechanism must assemble a landscape from real parts -- houses from one town, a church from another, a castle ruin -- and hold it together with one consistent light, so the viewer trusts the mood and never checks the map.

## M6. The half-hidden moon: light as a brief event

What it IS: a lighting device repeated across the nocturnes: the moon is small, often partly veiled by cloud, and its light arrives as a narrow event rather than a general illumination. National Gallery NG239: "The moon here has appeared only briefly, breaking through a tiny gap in a cloudy sky. The cool light creates a contrast between the deep darkness in the shadowy outlines of the town and the highlights on the rooftops and gable ends." Academia on the Getty picture: "The moon hangs to the right of center, a small, brilliant disc partly veiled by drifting clouds. Its light catches the edges of a great bank of white cloud that billows up behind the castle, and falls in a silvery path across the river toward us." Sotheby's Brookeriana: "The moon hangs low in the sky above one of Holland's inland waterways, its silvery light reflected on the river's still surface." How it shows: the moon is a small disc doing a large job -- one bright track across the water, lit cloud edges, everything else in warm dark. The picture is a moment -- the cloud gap -- not a state. A few minutes later, in the fiction of the picture, the light would be gone. -- National Gallery NG239 (read); Academia Moonlit Landscape (read); Sotheby's Brookeriana (read).

**Takeaway:** the light is a moment, not a condition. A piece built on this mechanism must make the moon small and half-veiled, its light a brief break in cloud -- a silvery track on the water that could close at any minute.

## M7. The warm/cool split: reddish storm against leaden cold

What it IS: a colour procedure: warm reddish-brown against cool blue-grey, used in both nocturnes and winter scenes. V&A: "the play of reddish brown and dark greyish blue." NG239: "The reddish hues are characteristic of van der Neer's style; they add a hint of storminess, a dramatic mood which is heightened here by a remarkably turbulent sky." Sotheby's Frozen River note: "Although the sky is blue and pink, lit by the setting sun, a range of cumulus clouds boiling on the horizon, these colors are barely reflected by the icy surface of the river. It is leaden in color, touched with shades of yellow and green, and chills us to look at." Sotheby's 2026 note: "Van der Neer has used a variety of warm brown and ochre tones to create a remarkable sense of space." How it shows: the sky is allowed to be warm and dramatic; the ground -- water or ice -- refuses to repeat it. The cold surface stays leaden while the sky burns. The split is the mood: warmth above, cold below, the viewer caught between them. -- V&A (read); National Gallery NG239 (read); Sotheby's Frozen River and 2026 Moonlit River Landscape notes (read).

**Takeaway:** warm sky, cold ground, and the split is the feeling. A piece built on this mechanism must let the sky burn reddish and stormy while the water or ice below stays leaden and refuses the warmth.

## M8. Frost without frostiness: ice that never feels frosty

What it IS: the signature winter effect, named by Britannica: "Another of his frequent subjects is frozen water, and his daylight icescapes with golfers, sleighers, and fishermen are about as numerous as his moonlight scenes. But he always avoids the impression of frostiness, which is one of his great gifts." The mechanism, from Sotheby's Frozen River note: the ice is painted leaden, not white -- "touched with shades of yellow and green" -- while the snow itself is bright white "on the ground, buildings and tree limbs," a brightness "which had largely disappeared in the more monochromatic landscapes of the intervening years" (Sotheby's, on his return to Avercamp). Mist rises from the ice in the distance, "blurring the windmill and the buildings around it, and finally disappearing into the cloud bank." How it shows: you feel the cold without being shown white glare -- the ice is a grey-green mirror, the snow is white only where it sits on things, and distance dissolves into mist. The picture chills by colour and by loss of detail, not by frost effects. -- Britannica via Wikipedia (read); Sotheby's Frozen River note (read).

**Takeaway:** cold without white. A piece built on this mechanism must paint ice leaden and yellow-green, keep the white for snow sitting on things, and let distance dissolve into mist -- the chill comes from colour and from detail giving up, never from frost effects.

## M9. The encyclopaedic winter: Avercamp's additive formula revived

What it IS: a compositional procedure borrowed from Hendrick Avercamp and his follower Anthonie Verstraelen, then revitalised. NGA (Winter in Holland entry, search result): Van der Neer's early winter paintings "tend to feature a frozen river receding into the distance with groupings of diversely dressed figures scattered across the picture plane," like Avercamp's. Thyssen (search result): "In some respects Van der Neer's compositional devices are old fashioned, returning to spatial formulae that Hendrick Avercamp had introduced in the first decade of the century. The latter's additive designs, which piled skaters up the surface of a frozen river stretching to a vanishing point on the central horizon, had been superseded by the winter scenes of Esaias van de Velde... But Van der Neer, who was older than either of these painters though something of a late bloomer, reverted to the time-honoured, encyclopaedic winter scenes with their community-wide compositions that Avercamp had popularised forty years earlier. However Van der Neer revitalised these retardataire designs with original contributions, namely an unprecedented responsiveness to coloured light and atmosphere." Sotheby's Frozen River note: "Moving away from the tonal landscapes and more focused depictions of his more immediate predecessors, Van der Neer returned instead to the broader views and compositional motifs that Avercamp had made famous earlier in the century." How it shows: the whole village is on the ice at once -- skaters, sledges, sleighs, fishermen, children, a kolf game in the foreground -- a census of winter life piled up the frozen river to the horizon. The old formula is the frame; the coloured light is the new content. -- NGA Winter in Holland entry and Thyssen entry (search results, cited verbatim); Sotheby's Frozen River note (read).

**Takeaway:** the whole village on the ice, in one old frame with new light. A piece built on this mechanism must pile a census of winter life up a frozen river to the horizon -- skaters, sledges, fishermen, a kolf game -- and let coloured light, not composition, be what is new in it.

## M10. The tiny figure as scale and life: staffage that proves the space

What it IS: a figure procedure: people are small, few, and placed to measure the landscape. NGA on Moonlit Landscape with Bridge: the moonlight "reveals paths and bridges, picks out the family returning home over the stone bridge with their dog, and highlights the elegant couple standing in the shadows of the trees at the right." Sotheby's Frozen River note: "On the road in the foreground stands an elegant couple, the father with his back to us looking at the figures on the frozen river, their son waiting patiently with his kolf club in his hand and their dog mesmerized by the figure passing in front of him holding a brace of waterfowl." National Gallery NG1288 (search result): "One man, about to strike the ball with his long club, is brightly lit in spite of the grey clouds... the man stands out in his white shirt sleeves and his kolfer's stance -- leaning forward, bottom out." And the Cuyp half of this mechanism: on joint pictures Cuyp supplied the figures and cattle (Britannica; Lempertz) -- Van der Neer outsourced the life when he wanted it livelier than his own hand could make it. How it shows: a figure with its back to us, a dog, a child with a kolf club -- each one is a measuring stick for the depth of the river and the height of the sky. The figures are small enough that the landscape stays the subject, large enough that the space becomes walkable. -- NGA Moonlit Landscape with Bridge (read); Sotheby's Frozen River note (read); National Gallery NG1288 and Britannica (search result / read).

**Takeaway:** the figure is a measuring stick, not a subject. A piece built on this mechanism must keep its people small and few -- a back turned to us, a dog, a child with a club -- placed so the depth of the river and the height of the sky can be read off them.

## M11. The fire picture: a burning town as a second sun

What it IS: a light system distinct from the moonlight pictures: a fire -- a burning village, the Amsterdam town hall, a blaze on the far bank -- becomes the picture's sun. Sotheby's 2011 lot note (search result): "in another painting of the same subject, from the collection of the Gemaldegalerie in Berlin, van der Neer has similarly used a river to draw the eye to the back of the composition, where the buildings of a town stand silhouetted against a powerful blaze." National Gallery artist page: he "also specialised in... paintings depicting fires." The fire pictures are the one place where Van der Neer paints a specific historical event rather than a mood: the 1652 town-hall fire (see leg 1 F9 for how far that claim survives -- Sotheby's correction: he painted fires; he is not documented as painting THE fire). How it shows: the river leads the eye to the blaze; the town is a black silhouette cut out of orange light; boats and residents flee or watch at the edges. The fire does what the moon does in the nocturnes -- it is the only light -- but warm, violent, and low, so the whole picture tilts toward it. -- Sotheby's 2011 lot note (search result); National Gallery artist page (read); Wikipedia (read).

**Takeaway:** the fire is the moon, but warm and violent. A piece built on this mechanism must put the blaze at the far end of the river, cut the town out of it as a black silhouette, and let the whole picture tilt toward the one warm light.

## M12. Serial variation: one formula, a hundred moods

What it IS: a working method and a market fact. Sotheby's 2026 note: "Despite the many evening and night scenes that comprise his oeuvre, each one is unique and displays a different mood." Academia Aesthetics: "He painted many close variants, so the same title is used for pictures in several museums." National Gallery NG239: "this is one of many he painted." The demand was so great that copies were made in his lifetime (Stadel: copies already in existence in 1664) and forgeries in the 18th and 19th centuries, "often inscribed with fake monograms" (V&A). The V&A's own *Moonlight and River Scene* is "unlikely an authentic work... a good example of his much copied oeuvre" and Schulz 2002 qualifies the attribution as questionable; *The Ferry: Moonlight* is "a probable 17th-century imitation." How it shows: the same river, the same moon, the same church-and-windmill silhouette -- but the cloud gap is in a different place, the moon higher or lower, the fire on the other bank. The viewer who has seen three Van der Neers starts comparing moods the way one compares evenings. -- Sotheby's 2026 note (read); Academia Aesthetics (read); National Gallery NG239 (read); V&A Moonlight and River Scene (read).

**Takeaway:** the same river, a different evening. A piece built on this mechanism must repeat one formula -- river, moon, church, windmill -- and vary only the cloud gap, the moon's height, the fire's bank, so the viewer compares moods the way one compares evenings.

## M13. Thin paint, jewel scale: small panels for collectors' cabinets

What it IS: a material fact across the oeuvre. Supports: oak panel (most nocturnes and winter scenes: NG239 and NG2536 "oil on wood (probably oak)"; Sotheby's Brookeriana "oil on oak panel"; Sotheby's Frozen River "oil on oak panel"); walnut panel, single piece, thin, horizontally grained (NGA Moonlit Landscape with Bridge); canvas for the larger pictures (Sotheby's 2026 Moonlit River Landscape, oil on canvas 87.6 x 110.5 cm; Bonhams evening landscape, oil on canvas 42.5 x 58 cm; Princeton River Landscape in Moonlight, oil on canvas 48.3 x 63 cm). Sizes: mostly small -- NG2536 is 19.7 x 28.3 cm; the cabinet pictures of the 1650s are "small, jewel-like paintings for collectors' cabinets" (Richard Green). Paint: "applied in thin layers" (NGA); "thin paint application in the darks" (Princeton); "The artist's original glazes, so easily disturbed by cleaning, remain intact throughout the landscape" (Sotheby's conservator). Monogram: AV DN interlaced in ligature, often with a date, sometimes scratched/etched into the paint (Princeton: monogram "Etched into the paint below the fencepost"). How it shows: the picture is a small, thin, glossy object that has to be stepped up to -- the light effects only resolve at close range, where the scratches, the stipple and the wood grain showing through become visible. The large canvases (87-115 cm) are the exceptions, not the rule. -- NGA technical entry (read); Princeton (read); Sotheby's Frozen River condition report (read); Richard Green (read); National Gallery NG239/NG2536 (read).

**Takeaway:** a jewel you step up to. A piece built on this mechanism must be small and thin and glossy, its light effects resolving only at close range -- the scratches, the stipple, the grain showing through -- with large scale kept as the rare exception.

## M14. The five-shilling economics: painting through poverty

What it IS: the economic condition of the whole career, documented and legendary at once. NGA: "Although there is no record of an amount paid for even a single painting by Aert van der Neer, it has long been believed that he did not command high prices." The tavern (1659-1662) and the bankruptcy (12 December 1662) are documented; the valuation of the estate pictures is the famous figure, in two currencies (see leg 1 F8): five guilders or less, from the bankruptcy inventory itself (NGA), against "about five shillings apiece" in the Britannica tradition. Bonhams: "It is a testament to his dedication as an artist, however, that van der Neer continued to paint while in a state of extreme poverty on the Kerkestraat in Amsterdam until his death." Stadel: copies of his work were already being inventoried in 1664 -- the market for the pictures existed before the painter was paid for them. And the posthumous reversal: Sotheby's kolf landscape (search result) carried an estimate of 1,000,000 - 1,500,000 GBP; "Van der Neer's nine most expensive paintings sold at auction are winter scenes." How it shows: the pictures themselves show no sign of the poverty -- they are patient, thin, jewel-finished, and numerous. The economics are invisible in the work and total in the life -- the mechanism is the gap between the two. -- NGA biography (read); Stadel (read); Bonhams (read); Sotheby's kolf landscape note (search result).

**Takeaway:** the work shows nothing of the poverty, and the poverty explains nothing in the work. A piece built on this mechanism must hold the two facts apart -- jewel-finished, patient, numerous pictures on one side; a bankrupt innkeeper on the other -- and let the gap itself be the subject.

## Works

## W1 -- Genre scene, 1632

Date 1632 (NGA biography, via Docslib full text of the NGA Online Edition entry by Arthur K. Wheelock Jr.). Medium not established in the material read. Dimensions NOT VERIFIED -- no dimension figure was found in the material read. Location: Narodni Galerie (National Gallery), Prague. The earliest known painting by Aert van der Neer, and the only genre scene in his oeuvre: "Van der Neer's earliest known painting, dated 1632 (Narodni Galerie, Prague), is a genre scene, a type of subject to which he never returned" (NGA biography). Everything after this is landscape. The picture is therefore the hinge of the whole career -- a painter who begins with people in an interior and then abandons people as a subject for forty-five years, keeping them only as staffage on ice and riverbanks. Do not confuse with the first dated landscape of 1633 (see B1).

## W2 -- Winter landscape, 1639 (Rijksmuseum)

Date 1639, monogram of A.V. and D.N. interlaced with the date. Medium not established in the material read. Dimensions NOT VERIFIED -- no dimension figure was found in the material read. Location: Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam. The earliest picture in which van der Neer coupled his interlaced monogram with a date, and the earliest dated winter landscape in the oeuvre. The old Britannica verdict, repeated verbatim in Wikipedia and in every secondary source read, calls it (with the 1642 Kiel picture) "immature works both, of poor quality". It is the baseline against which the 1643-45 breakthrough is measured: the same subject, the same monogram, three years later a different painter. The Rijksmuseum's own object page for this picture was not opened end to end, so medium and size are not stated here. (Do not attach the SK-C-191 inventory number to this picture: SK-C-191 is the Rijksmuseum's River View in Winter, 1655-1660, B18 -- see C618.)

## W3 -- Winter Landscape with Skaters, 1642 (Martins collection, Kiel)

Date 1642, monogram with date. Medium not established in the material read. Dimensions NOT VERIFIED. Location: Martins collection, Kiel (as cited in the Britannica/Wikipedia tradition). Present whereabouts not established in the material read. The second monogram-and-date picture, paired with W2 in every account as the other "immature" early work. The NGA scholarly article on W6 states the same sequence from the other end: "his first dated winter landscape came only in 1642". The Wikipedia works gallery illustrates a "Winter Landscape with Skaters" from a file dated c. 1642, which is very likely this picture, but the file page was not opened and the identification is not asserted here. What matters for evolution: between 1642 and 1645 the Avercamp-derived formula (high viewpoint, frozen river receding, figures scattered across the plane) is still in place; the break comes in 1643-45, not here.

## W4 -- Evening Landscape with Canal, 1643 (Schloss Friedenstein, Gotha)

Date 1643. Medium not established in the material read (Stechow catalogue lists it as "Evening landscape with canal", Ducal Museum Gotha, SG 707). Dimensions NOT VERIFIED -- no dimension figure was found in the material read. Location: Schloss Friedenstein (Ducal Museum), Gotha, inv. SG 707. Richard Green calls this "Van der Neer's first real evening landscape ... the painting of 1643 now in Schloss Friedenstein, Gotha", and the NGA biography agrees that "the earliest known of which [nocturnes] is dated 1643". The year 1643 is therefore the invention year: the first dated evening/night landscape, one year after the last "immature" winter picture. The picture itself is a dusk canal scene, not yet a full moonlight -- the moonlit formula is still a year away (W5, 1644). Treat 1643 as the start of the light-study, not its completion.

## W5 -- Moonlight Scene, 1644 (ex d'Arenberg collection, Brussels)

Date 1644. Medium not established in the material read. Dimensions NOT VERIFIED. Location: once in the d'Arenberg collection, Brussels (Britannica/Wikipedia). Present whereabouts not established in the material read. "Far better is the Winter Landscape (1643), and the Moonlight Scene (1644) once in the d'Arenberg collection in Brussels" -- the Britannica sentence that has carried the early chronology for a century. The 1644 Moonlight Scene is the first named moonlight picture, and the hinge between the evening landscape of 1643 and the great moonlit river landscapes of 1646-50. Because its present whereabouts are unknown, it cannot be checked against the later pictures; it survives in the literature as a date and a title only. Do not conflate it with any surviving "Moonlight Scene" in a museum listing.

## W6 -- Winter in Holland: Skating Scene, 1645

Date 1645, signed and dated lower left in monogram, first and last two letters in ligature: AV DN / 1645. Medium: oil on panel (two quarter-sawn oak panels, horizontal grain, butt-joined along the centre). Dimensions 55 x 70.8 cm (21 5/8 x 27 7/8 in.) -- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED (the figure is from the NGA object record via search result and the Docslib full text of the NGA entry; the NGA scholarly article read end to end, 73 lines, gives the date, medium discussion and technical analysis but no dimensions in its extracted text). Location: National Gallery of Art, Washington, D.C., acc. 2014.136.37 (Corcoran Collection, William A. Clark Collection, bequest 1926; acquired by the NGA 2014). The fullest-documented winter picture in this leg, and the transitional work of the career. The NGA entry (Anchin/Gifford, read end to end): a bright midday scene on a winding frozen river, men and women skating, sledding, riding horse-drawn sleighs, a game of colf (kolf) in the foreground, a handsome couple in their finest greeting friends at the riverbank. It "belongs to a transitional moment": the early Avercamp/Verstraelen formula (single-bank view, high viewpoint, low horizon) is retained, but "the recession of space is achieved more gradually, and the figures are more numerous and densely grouped... distributed horizontally across the foreground, effecting a gentler integration of the human presence within the space of the landscape". Infrared reflectography shows the struggle: figures around a short craggy tree painted out or never completed, to open the path in the left foreground. Technique, from the NGA examination: off-white ground plus an extremely thin tan layer used as base colour; trees and cottage sides articulated with touches of brown; snow as white accents; a thin pale blue-gray brushed across the tan layer for the shimmery translucency of ice and frost; selective white impasto for untrodden snow; paint thin and predominantly wet-into-wet, figures delineated first in black paint on top of the landscape. The entry dates the winter-landscape turn to Verstraelen's death in 1641 -- "With the absence of Verstraelen, Van der Neer may have seen an opportunity to make his foray into the winter genre" -- and notes that around this same time he was "also developing a specialty in nocturnes".

## W7 -- A River near a Town, by Moonlight, c. 1645

Date about 1645 (National Gallery). Medium: oil on wood (probably oak). Dimensions 30.3 x 48.4 cm -- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED (National Gallery object listing via search result; the nationalgallery.org.uk object page was not opened end to end). Location: National Gallery, London, NG239 (bequeathed by Lord Colborne, 1854). A small moonlight, and the clearest statement of the mature formula in miniature: "The moon here has appeared only briefly, breaking through a tiny gap in a cloudy sky. The cool light creates a contrast between the deep darkness in the shadowy outlines of the town and the highlights on the rooftops and gable ends" (National Gallery). The moon is not a disc in a clear sky but a brief break in cloud -- the light arrives as an event, not a condition. At 30.3 x 48.4 cm it is a cabinet nocturne, roughly half the size of the contemporary winter pictures, which is the pattern for the whole 1640s: moonlights are painted small, winters larger.

## W8 -- A Village by a River in Moonlight, c. 1645

Date about 1645 (National Gallery). Medium: oil on wood (probably oak). Dimensions 19.7 x 28.3 cm -- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED (National Gallery object listing via search result; the object page was not opened end to end). Location: National Gallery, London, NG2536 (Salting Bequest, 1910). The smallest work in the 20, and the purest demonstration that the moonlight formula does not need size: "It probably doesn't depict a 'real' place, but one composed by the artist so that he could highlight the reflections of light on the mirror-still water, and the carefully arranged silhouettes of the church, the windmill and the boats lying off the quayside" (National Gallery). Church, windmill, boats -- three silhouettes, one still river, one moon. The composition is a kit of parts arranged for reflection, and the National Gallery's wording ("composed... so that he could highlight") is the key to van der Neer's whole method: the place is invented to serve the light, not recorded. Compare W10, where the same kit is deployed at three times the size around a real landmark.

## W9 -- River View by Moonlight, c. 1646

Date c. 1646 (Richard Green, citing the Six Collection provenance) / c. 1650-1655 (Google Arts and Culture) / 1645 (WahooArt and related listings). Unresolved -- see Disputes. Medium: oil on panel. Dimensions 55 x 103 cm -- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED (WahooArt and related listings; the Rijksmuseum object page was not opened end to end). Location: Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam (ex Six Collection). Richard Green: "His first really remarkable paintings of this kind are the moonlit river landscape of circa 1646 from the Six Collection, now in the Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam, and the townscape by moonlight in the Museum Bredius at The Hague." The wide format (55 x 103 cm, nearly 1:2) is the first panoramic moonlight in the set -- the river is allowed to run the full width of the picture, and the moonlight has a long horizontal surface to work on. Sotheby's (2012, lot 22 note) adds that the companion Bischoffsheim moonlit river landscape, "formerly in the collection of King Willem II of The Netherlands, is now in the Rijksmuseum" -- very likely this picture, though Sotheby's does not give the inventory number. The dating spread (1645 to c. 1655) is the widest in the 20 and is flagged, not resolved.

## W10 -- Moonlit Landscape with a View of the New Amstel River and Castle Kostverloren, 1647

Date 1647, monogrammed in ligature and dated on the tree stump (extreme right): AVDN 1647. Wikipedia gives 1647-49. Medium: oil on panel. Dimensions 57.5 x 89.9 cm (22 5/8 x 35 3/8 in.) -- VERIFIED (EN Wikipedia article read end to end, 123 lines, L4; confirmed by the Getty CONA record via search result, unframed 57.5 x 89.9 cm). Location: J. Paul Getty Museum, Getty Center, Los Angeles, acc. 2000.27. The dated anchor of the moonlight series and the one picture in the 20 built around a named, real landmark: Kostverloren, a castle on the Amstel just outside Amsterdam, "here seen as a pale silhouette among trees" (Academia Aesthetics, read end to end). The composition is the formula at full strength: a river running into the distance between dark banks, the moon half-hidden by cloud setting the edges of the clouds and the water alight, a ruined castle as the pale centre-distance accent. Sotheby's (2012) cites a second Kostverloren moonlight of 1646 in the Israel Museum, Jerusalem (Schulz no. 377) -- the same ruin painted twice in two years, which makes Kostverloren the one topographical motif van der Neer demonstrably returned to. The 1647 date is inscribed, not estimated, which makes this the firmest fixed point in the whole chronology after 1645.

## W11 -- Moonlit Landscape with Bridge, probably 1648/1650

Date probably 1648/1650 (NGA). Medium: oil on panel -- a single piece of thin, horizontally grained walnut, cut across the entire tree trunk width. Dimensions 78.4 x 110.2 cm (30 7/8 x 43 3/8 in.); framed 101.6 x 132.7 cm -- VERIFIED (EN Wikipedia article read end to end, 123 lines, L41-42; confirmed by the NGA object record via search result. The NGA scholarly article read end to end, 46 lines, gives the technique but the dimensions in its extracted text come via the object record). Location: National Gallery of Art, Washington, D.C., acc. 1990.6.1 (Patrons' Permanent Fund), West Building Gallery M49, on display. The largest moonlight in the 20 and the culmination of the 1640s nocturne series: "Although few of Van der Neer's paintings are dated, broad patterns within his stylistic development suggest that he executed this work near the end of the 1640s. It stands at the culmination of a period when his nocturnal scenes depicted the reflections of a full moon in the quiet waterways of the Dutch countryside" (NGA scholarly article, read end to end). Full moon diffused by varied cloud, strong reflections in the slow river and on the windows of the shore buildings, gnarled trunks at left brought to life, a family returning home over the stone bridge with their dog, an elegant couple in the shadows of the trees at right. The setting is explicitly a composite -- Amsterdam-like houses at left, small-town church beyond, a ruined castle or country house across the river, a Vecht-style stone gate at right -- "a composite of various aspects of city and country life that Van der Neer has brought into a harmonious whole". Technique, from the NGA article: reddish ocher imprimatura left visible through thinly applied bluish-gray paint for the translucency of the clouds; light contours articulated sometimes with the brush, sometimes by scratching the wet paint with an instrument to reveal the ground layer; stippling in foliage and lawn; brushwork prominent in the sky; the walnut grain plainly visible through a smooth, extremely thin white ground. The article also gives the genre's pedigree: moonlit landscape in the Netherlands traces to Hendrik Goudt's engravings after Adam Elsheimer in the 1610s, and Rafael Camphuysen was one of the few Dutch painters to venture into it before van der Neer -- but "it seems that he developed his style rather independently", and "only Peter Paul Rubens explored the transformation of a landscape through light as profoundly as did Van der Neer". Flag: WahooArt gives the dimensions as 102 x 133 cm, which is the framed size (101.6 x 132.7 cm), not the panel -- see Disputes.

## W12 -- A Frozen River near a Village, with Golfers and Skaters, c. 1648

Date about 1648 (National Gallery). Medium not established in the material read (the National Gallery listing in the search extract gives no medium; other listings say oil on panel, but that is not asserted here). Dimensions NOT VERIFIED -- no dimension figure appears in the National Gallery extract read, and no other reliable figure was found. Location: National Gallery, London, NG1288. The daylight icescape as social theatre: "European winters in the mid-seventeenth century were severe. Rivers and canals froze so solid that the life normally lived in a village moved out onto the frozen waterways and carried on -- business as usual. But van der Neer shows his villagers at play." One man about to strike the ball with his long club is brightly lit in spite of the grey clouds; couples play along the far bank; skaters tip crazily; a sledge carries an old woman bundled in her shawl; a fisherman checks his lines; a woman sits on a beached boat rubbing her leg. The National Gallery's own text corrects the old Britannica line: "Starting as a landscape painter, van der Neer came to winter scenes quite late in his career" -- late here meaning the mid-1640s, after the tonal landscapes of the early 1630s. It also closes with the life fact the pictures never show: "Sadly, van der Neer's painting failed to keep him solvent. He kept an inn and painted in his spare time, but even that wasn't enough to make him a livelihood and he died bankrupt."

## W13 -- A Landscape with a River at Evening, c. 1650

Date about 1650 (National Gallery). Medium: oil on canvas. Dimensions 79 x 65.1 cm -- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED (National Gallery object listing via search result, 79 x 65.1 cm; the FeelTheArt listing gives the same figures reversed as 0.651 x 0.79 m, confirming the pair. The object page was not opened end to end). Location: National Gallery, London, NG2283 (bequeathed by Martin H. Colnaghi, 1908). The evening picture as a problem of naming: "The title given to this painting (probably in the eighteenth or nineteenth century) suggests that it's set in the evening, though the cold blues and yellows of the sky could equally well suggest dawn" (National Gallery). The brightest point is on the horizon, right in the centre, backlighting the whole scene, silhouetting a battleship anchored in the mouth of the estuary and casting the wood into deep shadow. It is the only work in the 20 with a ship of that scale as the dark centre -- a reminder that van der Neer's "evening" pictures shade into marine painting, and that the hour is deliberately unfixable: the same cold blues and yellows serve dawn and dusk alike. The vertical format (79 cm high against 65.1 wide) is unusual in an oeuvre built on wide horizontals, and it is earned by the centred horizon light, which needs height above it for the sky to work.

## W14 -- The Farrier, early or mid-1650s

Date early or mid-1650s (Met). Medium: oil on wood. Dimensions 48.3 x 61.3 cm (19 x 24 1/8 in.) -- VERIFIED (Met object page read end to end, 60 lines, L16). Location: Metropolitan Museum of Art, New York, acc. 71.60 (Purchase, 1871). The double-light picture: "Van der Neer's preoccupation with light effects in nature led him to paint dozens of nocturnal views. In this case the warm light of a forge and a bonfire set off the cool glow and reflections of the moon. Recent conservation treatment has revealed the buttery yellow of the moon and the bright orange glow of the forge" (Met). Moonlight and firelight in one frame, cool against warm -- the two light sources the whole career alternates between, here combined. The farrier at his forge is the excuse for the orange; the moon is the excuse for the blue; the picture is the contest between them. It is also the only work in the 20 where a trade (shoeing a horse) is the foreground action rather than skating, fishing or walking home -- labour as a light source, not as a subject.

## W15 -- Winter Landscape with Kolf Players (Greffulhe), c. 1650 / early 1650s

Date c. 1650 or early 1650s (Schulz, via Sotheby's). Medium: oil on oak panel, with an old cradle. Dimensions 57 x 83.5 cm (22 3/8 x 32 7/8 in.) -- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED (Sotheby's lot page via search result; the lot page was not opened end to end). Location: private collection (ex Comte Henri Greffulhe, acquired 1868 through Francois Nieuwenhuys for 215 guineas; Sotheby's New York, 24 January 2008, lot 17, $2.05 million). "Few of Van der Neer's paintings are dated, but this work, dated circa 1650 or to the early 1650s by Wolfgang Schulz, is a superb example of his early maturity" (Sotheby's). The kolf game -- the Dutch ice game between hockey and golf -- is the foreground action, and the panel is the standard mature winter format: 57 x 83.5 cm, oak, wide, with the view receding to a distant walled town. The provenance is a small history of the taste for van der Neer: bought for 215 guineas in 1868, sold for $2.05 million in 2008. The picture was catalogued by Bachmann (1982, pl. 108) and Schulz (2002); its price, not its size, is what changed most between the two catalogues.

## W16 -- Winter Landscape with Figures Battling across a Frozen River in a Snowstorm, c. 1655-60

Date c. 1655-60 (Sutton and van Suchtelen, from the costumes); Schulz dates it "around 1660?". See Disputes. Medium: oil on canvas, signed with monogram lower centre AV DN (in ligature). Dimensions 60.8 x 75.7 cm (24 x 29 3/4 in.) -- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED (Sotheby's lot 40, 2008, via search result and the Invaluable mirror; the lot page was not opened end to end). Location: private collection (Sotheby's, 2008, lot 40). The most extreme weather picture in the oeuvre, and the one Sotheby's calls "one of Van der Neer's greatest and most atmospheric winter landscapes". The winter here is not passive -- ice, lying snow and frost -- but active: "wind, moving clouds and driving snow. Here we see a blizzard in which snowflakes are driven across the picture from right to left by a strong wind which bends the bare trees". Van Suchtelen's observation, quoted by Sotheby's: "Van der Neer is the only Dutch 17th-century painter to dare to depict falling snow". A post mill stands to the right; figures battle across the frozen river. Chosen for both the landmark Masters of 17th Century Dutch Landscape Painting exhibition (1987-88) and the Mauritshuis Holland Frozen in Time show (2001-02, no. 17). The snowstorm is the winter equivalent of the moon breaking through cloud in W7 -- the weather as an event crossing the picture, not a backdrop.

## W17 -- Winter Scene with Figures Playing Kolf, 1655-1660

Date 1655-1660 (Norton Simon). Medium not established in the material read (the Norton Simon extract gives no medium). Dimensions 57.8 x 78.1 cm (22 3/4 x 30 3/4 in.) -- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED (Norton Simon object listing via search result; the object page was not opened end to end). Location: Norton Simon Museum, Pasadena, acc. F.1972.35.P. The Norton Simon's reading of the late winter pictures is the clearest statement of what the subject became: "As such, the tinted light of the setting sun, as it is refracted by the clouds and moist atmosphere and reflects on the neutral landscape, provides the true subject of this enchanting painting." The kolf players are staffage; the tinted light is the subject. The dimensions (57.8 x 78.1 cm) are within a centimetre of W15 (57 x 83.5 cm) -- the mature winter format has stabilised at roughly 58 x 80 cm, and the variation between pictures is now in the light, not the size. Compare W6 (55 x 70.8 cm, 1645): fifteen years on, the format has barely moved; the handling of light has moved everything.

## W18 -- A Frozen River by a Town at Evening, c. 1665

Date about 1665 (National Gallery). Medium: oil on wood (probably oak). Dimensions 26.4 x 40.5 cm -- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED (National Gallery object listing via search result; the object page was not opened end to end. The FeelTheArt listing wrongly calls it oil on canvas -- see Disputes). Location: National Gallery, London, NG969 (Wynn Ellis Bequest, 1876). "This image of an unknown town is one of dozens of similar landscapes which van der Neer made around this time" (National Gallery). Dark clouds tinged with russet from an unseen sunset billow up like smoke from behind the buildings. The small size (26.4 x 40.5 cm) against the late date matters: in the mid-1660s, alongside the huge canvases of W19 and W20, van der Neer is still painting the small cabinet winter on panel -- the two scales run in parallel, not in sequence. Bonhams (2017) notes a "very similar composition, for example, with the church, the windmill and a boat in the foreground, a winter landscape on panel, 26.4 x 40.5 cm., is in the National Gallery, London" -- the same measurements, cited as the comparison piece for a canvas evening landscape. The National Gallery's dating (c. 1665) also makes this the latest dated National Gallery van der Neer in the set, and the one Sotheby's (2011) uses as the comparison for a c. 1660 winter landscape: "this picture may be compared to Wide Frozen River Landscape in the Evening Near a Town, from around the same moment, and now in the National Gallery, London (inv. no. 969)".

## W19 -- A View along a River near a Village at Evening, 1660s

Date 1660s (National Gallery). Medium: oil on canvas, signed. Dimensions 133.5 x 167.5 cm -- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED (National Gallery object listing via search result; the object page was not opened end to end). Location: National Gallery, London, NG732 (bought, 1864). "This is one of Aert van der Neer's largest paintings. A river takes centre stage, stretching into the middle distance beyond and reflecting back the slanting light of an evening sky and the darker shadows of the boats and trees" (National Gallery). At 133.5 x 167.5 cm it is more than five times the area of W18 and the largest work in this leg -- the scale jump of the late career, from cabinet panel to a canvas over a metre and a half wide. The National Gallery notes "some evidence from early sales catalogues that it may be a view of Maarssen, which is on the River Vecht and about five miles from Utrecht" -- a rare topographical suggestion for a painter whose places are normally invented. The Stadel biography is the gloss on the size: "Large-format works suggest patrons, but these have not been documented." A canvas this big, in the 1660s, from a painter who died bankrupt, is a document of ambition without a documented buyer.

## W20 -- A Winter Landscape with Figures in the Foreground Collecting Wood, c. 1670

Date c. 1670 (Schulz). Medium: oil on canvas, signed with monogram AV DN (in ligature, lower right). Dimensions 97 x 109 cm (38 1/8 x 42 7/8 in.) -- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED (Bonhams lot pages, 2009 and later sale, via search result; the lot page was not opened end to end). Location: private collection (Bonhams, 9 December 2009, lot 8; previously Baron Edmond de Beurnonville, Paris; sale Amsterdam, de Beuckelaer, 16 September 1739, lot 133). The late apogee, in Schulz's own words: "The dramatic atmosphere of the scene in this huge winter landscape is remarkable and nearly unique in Dutch representations of the winter. The picture can be seen as the final development in the artist's winter landscapes in the decades from 1640 till 1670. The painting, a late, ripe work around 1670, represents the apogee of Aert van der Neer's artistic work." Figures collect wood in the foreground, skaters and a village beyond; the staffage is "especially large... peculiarly broad and vigorously painted with a Rembrandtesque brush" (Bredius, 1900, describing this picture as he saw it in the Brakke Grond). Hofstede de Groot catalogued it in 1923 as by Jan van de Capelle (vol. VII, no. 177); Stechow (1966) removed it from Capelle; Bachmann (1982) listed it as a lost work; Schulz (2002, no. 91) restored it to van der Neer with a certificate of authentication dated 16 December 2003. The attribution history is the picture's second subject: a late, large, broadly painted winter that spent a century under another name. If Schulz's c. 1670 date holds, it is the latest securely dated work in the 20 -- painted in the same years as the bankruptcy's aftermath, and seven years before his death.

## Beyond the 20

B1. First dated landscape, 1633 -- Richard Green: "his first known work dates from 1632... In 1633 he worked in Amsterdam with Jochem Camphuysen... His first dated landscape is from the following year [1633]". Title, medium, dimensions and location not established in the material read; not in the 20 for that reason. The 1632 genre scene (W1) and the 1633 landscape together fix the start of the career.
B2. First commissioned landscape, 1635, of large dimension -- Richard Green: "In 1635 van der Neer painted his first commissioned landscape, of large dimension." Title, location and dimensions not established in the material read. The earliest evidence that large format (cf. W19, W20) was a commission-driven exception from the start, not a late development.
B3. River in Winter, 1645 -- oil on canvas, 36 x 62 cm; Hermitage, St Petersburg (WahooArt/ArtsDot listings). A small early winter, contemporary with W6, in the museum Britannica calls the choicest selection of his work.
B4. Landscape with Windmill, 1646 -- oil on panel, 69 x 92 cm; Hermitage, St Petersburg (WahooArt/ArtsDot listings). Dated 1646, the same year as W9; the Hermitage pair (B3, B4) shows both subjects -- winter and moonlit windmill -- running side by side in the mid-1640s.
B5. Moonlit Estuary, c. 1640-1650 -- oil on panel, 23 x 38 cm; Harvard Art Museums, acc. 2016.239 (Gift of Peter and Anne Brooke). "Van der Neer delineated moonlight on the clouds, distant hillsides, and still water using thin, soft brushwork and minuscule dabs of paint. Boats, cottages, and a church tower are silhouetted against the cloudy sky, whose colors change from orange-pink and light blue to dark bluish-gray as the sun sets and the moon rises" (Harvard). Former title: Landscape in Twilight -- the same dawn/dusk ambiguity as W13.
B6. River Landscape in Moonlight, c. 1645-55 -- oil on canvas, 48.3 x 63 cm; Princeton University Art Museum, acc. y1959-134 (Gift of Edwin H. Herzog). Provenance runs through the Rothschild collection in Vienna, seized by the Nazis in March 1938, held at Alt Aussee, returned via the Munich Central Collecting Point in 1945-46. "A church spire and castle in the distance lead the viewer's eye to the horizon" (Princeton).
B7. Moonlit landscape, signed and dated 1647 -- oil on oak panel, 83.8 x 114.9 cm; private collection (Sotheby's, Bibliotheca Brookeriana, 4 December 2025). Signed and dated lower left AV DN 1647; Schulz no. 1188 lists it as dated 1645 -- see Disputes. "The moon hangs low in the sky above one of Holland's inland waterways, its silvery light reflected on the river's still surface" (Sotheby's). The largest dated moonlight in the material read.
B8. Moonlit River Landscape, late 1640s -- oil on canvas, 87.6 x 110.5 cm; private collection (Sotheby's, 2 June 2026). Signed in monogram lower right; ex Lulworth Castle (Joseph Weld, by 1967); Schulz no. 615, "as probably authentic". Schulz endorsed the attribution "on the basis of photographs" and dated it to the late 1640s; "From 1643 onwards, Van der Neer occupied himself intensively with the phenomena of illumination and light... The second half of the 1640s was also the most productive period in Van der Neer's life" (Sotheby's).
B9. A Moonlit Landscape with a Burning Village -- Sotheby's, 2011, lot 171; Schulz no. 1380, reproduced in colour plate 62. Private collection, Germany, by 1984; exhibited Berlin, Gemaldegalerie, 1984, no. 47. Dimensions not in the extract read. Sotheby's: the 1652 town-hall fire "may well have inspired the depictions of fiery landscapes within his oeuvre, including the current work", and cites the Berlin Gemaldegalerie version of the same subject (Schulz no. 1357) -- see Disputes.
B10. Fire at Amsterdam by Night (Fire in Amsterdam by Night), undated -- oil on canvas, 58.8 x 71.7 cm; Statens Museum for Kunst, Copenhagen (Eclectic Light, citing Wikimedia Commons). A broad canal at night, residents taking to boats in case of evacuation, the fire in the distance. The Copenhagen picture of the Britannica's "two or three pictures, now in the galleries of Berlin and Copenhagen" -- see Disputes.
B11. Landscape at Sunset, 1650 -- oil on canvas, 51 x 71 cm; Metropolitan Museum of Art, New York (ArtsDot/Wikioo listings). A sunset river, contemporary with W13; the Met's second van der Neer after W14.
B12. River Landscape at Sunset, 1650 -- oil on canvas, 46 x 63 cm; Mauritshuis, The Hague (TopImpressionists listing). A second 1650 sunset, smaller than B11, in the museum that holds the winter-landscape exhibition history of W16.
B13. Sports on a Frozen River, c. 1660 -- oil, 23 x 35 cm; Metropolitan Museum of Art, New York (WahooArt/ArtsDot listings). A small late winter, the same title and decade as the NiceArtGallery's "Sports on a Frozen River (c. 1648)" -- the dating spread for one title is flagged in Disputes.
B14. Winter Landscape with Ice Skaters, c. 1660 -- 47 x 81 cm; Museum Boijmans Van Beuningen, Rotterdam, acc. VdV 56 (loan Willem van der Vorm Foundation, 1972). Medium not stated in the extract read.
B15. Winter Landscape with Sportsmen on a Frozen River, c. 1660 -- 13 3/8 x 15 7/8 in. (c. 34 x 40.3 cm); private collection (Sotheby's, 2011, lot 37). "After 1646, none of van der Neer's winter landscapes are dated" (Sotheby's, citing Schulz); datable to c. 1660, and "by around 1660 winter landscapes dominated van der Neer's output".
B16. A winter landscape with figures and horse-drawn sleighs on a frozen canal in a town by moonlight, late 1660s -- oil on canvas, signed in monogram lower right AVDN; private collection (Sotheby's, 2006, lot 94). Schulz dates it to the late 1660s (no. 122); Hofstede de Groot vol. VII, no. 583. A moonlit winter -- the two subjects combined in one picture, with a triple-arched bridge and a church behind. Dimensions not in the extract read.
B17. Young Men Playing "Kolf" in a Winter Landscape, c. 1665-1675 -- oil on canvas, 47.5 x 58.5 cm; Williams College Museum of Art, acc. 79.1 (Museum purchase, Wunsch Americana Foundation). The latest-dated kolf picture in the material read, and evidence the winter subject continued into the 1670s.
B18. River View in Winter, 1655-1660 -- oil on canvas, 64 x 79 cm; Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam, SK-C-191 (acquired 1885, ex Van der Hoop). "Op het ijs van een breed water buiten een stad vermaken mensen zich met kolfspelen, schaatsen en rijden in de arrenslede. Op de voorgrond een eendenkooi, rechts in de verte een molen, links de torens van de stad" (Commons file record, citing the Rijksmuseum). Dimensions from the Commons file record (64 x 79 cm); the FeelTheArt listing gives 64 x 55.5 cm, which conflicts -- see Disputes.
B19. Winter Landscape, c. 1655-1660 -- 23.4 x 33.9 cm; Alte Pinakothek, Munich, acc. 6383 (Bavarian State Painting Collections). A small late winter on panel scale, contemporary with the large canvases -- the two scales running in parallel (cf. W18).
B20. Winter landscape with skaters (Amstel, old town gate), 1660s -- oil on canvas, signed lower right in double monogram; private collection (Sotheby's, 2012, lot 22). Hofstede de Groot vol. VII, no. 10: "the picture may be regarded as the most important of its kind"; "The colouring is brilliant and the movement of the figures is convincingly rendered". Hofstede de Groot identified the view as the Amstel with the old town gate (demolished 1841); Sotheby's cautions that "such topographical exactitude would, however, be unusual for Van der Neer". Exhibited Amsterdam Winterlandschappen 1932 no. 57; Detroit Institute of Arts (lent by D. Katz). Dimensions not in the extract read. Schulz no. 196, "as present whereabouts unknown" before the sale.
B21. Winter landscape (Mills), c. 1643-4 / c. 1645 / 1655-60 -- fine oak panel with no joints, bevelled on all sides; private collection (Sotheby's, 2013, lot 22). Unknown to scholars until Sotheby's 1975, when it fetched the then-unprecedented 110,000 pounds; exhibited Mauritshuis Holland Frozen in Time 2001-02, no. 16. "Painted in muted tones of grey and brown... this is a yellow-brown peaty river, and its earthy tone is closer to the plaster and brick of the buildings than to the sky" (Sotheby's). Dating dispute -- see Disputes. Dimensions not in the extract read.
B22. River landscape by Moonlight, c. 1677 -- oil on canvas; Weston Park Foundation, Shropshire (Bridgeman Images, via Meisterdrucke). Listed by Meisterdrucke among the notable works as "River landscape by Moonlight (1677)". The 1677 date -- the year of his death -- is not corroborated by any scholarly source in the material read and conflicts with the V&A's "latest from 1665"; flagged in Disputes, not asserted.
B23. Moonlight and River Scene, c. 1635-1677 -- oil on oak panel, 33.2 x 49.5 cm; Victoria and Albert Museum, London, acc. DYCE.7 (Bequeathed by Rev. Alexander Dyce, 1869). "This painting is unlikely an authentic work by Aert van der Neer but a good example of his much copied oeuvre and the attribution to the artist is qualified in the catalogue raisonne (Schulz, 2002) as questionable" (V&A). The demand for his moonlights in 1770-80 and 1815-40 "led to a series of copies and forgeries often inscribed with fake monograms" (V&A). Kept in the list as the control case: what a van der Neer looks like when it is not one.
B24. A River Scene: Evening, probably late 1640s; Canal Scene by Moonlight, c. 1645-c. 1650; A Winter Scene, mid-1650s; A Skating Scene, c. 1655-c. 1660 -- four works, Wallace Collection, London (PaintingsBefore1800 visit notes; Art UK listings). Dimensions and media not established in the material read. The Wallace group is the best-preserved single-collection sample of the whole range -- evening, moonlight canal, winter, skating -- in one house. Two further Wallace listings are later copies/after: "Scene on a Canal, c. 1680-1780 (after)" and "A River Scene by Moonlight, probably 18th C (style of)" -- see Disputes.
B25. Landscape at Sunset, Louvre (joint work with Aelbert Cuyp) -- "We are near Dordrecht in the landscape sunset of the Louvre, in which Cuyp evidently painted the foreground and cows" (Britannica/Wikipedia). Dimensions, medium and inventory number not established in the material read. The joint-work tradition also names: the National Gallery picture in which "Cuyp signs his name on the pail of a milkmaid, whose figure and red skirt he has painted" (probably W13 or a related evening landscape -- the identification is not fixed in the material read); and "a couple of fishermen with a dog, and a sportsman creeping up to surprise some ducks, are Cuyp's in a capital Van der Neer at the Staedel Institute in Frankfurt" (Britannica). See Disputes.
B26. An Evening Landscape with Fishermen in the Foreground, figures resting beside a bridge -- oil on canvas, 42.5 x 58 cm; private collection (Bonhams, 6 December 2017, lot 48; sold 56,250 pounds). Signed with monogram AV DN lower right; Hofstede de Groot vol. VII, no. 322; Schulz no. 990, "Attribution plausible" and "possibly the copy sold in Amsterdam, 12 October 1774, lot 352" -- Bonhams argues the description and dimensions match Hofstede de Groot no. 322 exactly. Provenance: sale Amsterdam 1774, lot 352 (fl. 121); Leonard Koetser, London, 1958 catalogue no. 6 (dimensions incorrectly given as 42.5 x 50 cm).
B27. Winter Landscape with Houses, c. 1645-1650 -- 25 x 36.5 cm; location not established in the material read (Google Arts and Culture; provenance Theodor Lang, Paris; Jules Porges, Paris; F. Kleinberger Gallery, Paris; A. de Ridder and heirs, Kronberg, 1910-1924; purchased with the support of the Rembrandt Association and private individuals, 1924). A small early winter in the W6-W8 size class.
B28. Snowfall on a village beside a frozen canal, 1630-1677 -- oil on canvas, 53.5 x 41.5 cm; Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam (FeelTheArt listing, AI-generated description -- flagged). "A frozen canal dominates the foreground and middle ground, with numerous figures skating and walking on the ice" -- the Rijksmuseum's snowstorm winter (cf. W16), dated only to the whole career in the listing. Dimensions and dating both need a museum-page check.

Beyond-the-20 count: 28.

Further names found but not counted as separate works above (mentioned in sources, insufficiently documented to list): *A Village on a River by Moonlight*, 1640, oil on panel (Meisterdrucke listing); *Moonlit Landscape with a Windmill*, 1650, oil on oak panel (Meisterdrucke listing); *River landscape with Moonshine*, 1655 (Meisterdrucke listing); *Village hollandais au bord d'un canal*, 1665 and *Mondscheinlandschaft mit einer Stadt an breitem Kanal*, 1654 (Meisterdrucke notable-works list); *Harbour by Moonlight*, after 1665 (PubHist listing); *Winter Scene at Sunset with a Beacon to the Left*, Wallace Collection, inv. P-127 (cited by Sotheby's as Schulz's comparison piece for Frozen River at Sunset -- dimensions not established); *Winter Scene on a Frozen Canal near a Town on the Left Bank*, *Winter Scene in the Evening near a Village with a Ruin to the Left*, *River Landscape by Moonlight with a Ferryboat to the Right* (PubHist titles, no locations); *An Evening Landscape with a Horse and Cart by a Stream*, National Gallery (Gallerix title page, no inventory number in the extract); *An Evening View near a Village*, c. 1650, National Gallery -- the FeelTheArt listing gives 162.5 x 121.5 cm, which would make it the second-largest work in the material read, but the listing is AI-generated and the figure is NOT trusted here; *A River Landscape with a Village*, probably c. 1645, NG2534, oil on wood (National Gallery listing via search; dimensions not in the extract); *Winter Landscape with Figures on a Frozen Canal*, c. 1653-1656, Guildhall Art Gallery (Art UK listing); *A Town on a Frozen River*, 1645/1650, National Trust, Polesden Lacey (Art UK listing); *Moonlight Landscape (imitator)*, 74 x 61 cm, Alte Pinakothek (FeelTheArt listing, AI-generated -- an imitator, not counted).

## Evolution

Periods used here (from the NGA biography and the NGA scholarly articles, read end to end, and the Richard Green / Stadel biographies via search): Apprenticeship and tonal landscape c. 1632-1642; the invention of the evening and moonlight picture 1643-1646; maturity and the most productive years, second half of the 1640s to mid-1650s; winter dominance and large format c. 1660-1670; last years to 1677, painting probably until the beginning of the 1670s. The break is dated, not gradual, in the sources: the first dated winter landscape 1642, the first real evening landscape 1643, the first named moonlight 1644, the most productive period the second half of the 1640s.

What changed -- subject. Early (W1-W3, 1632-42): a genre scene, then tonal landscapes and winter scenes in the manner of Rafael Camphuysen, Esaias van de Velde and Hendrick Avercamp -- realistic, high-viewpoint, figures scattered across a frozen river. Middle (W4-W13, 1643-50): the invention. The evening landscape (W4, 1643), the moonlight (W5, 1644), then the two subjects running in parallel -- bright midday winter (W6, 1645) and small cabinet moonlights (W7, W8, c. 1645), the wide panoramic moonlight (W9, c. 1646), the named-landmark moonlight (W10, 1647), the culminating full-moon bridge picture (W11, 1648/50). The fire pictures (B9, B10) belong here too, after the 1652 town-hall fire -- a third light source, fire, added to moon and sun. Late (W14-W20, 1650s-1670): the moonlight does not disappear but the winter takes over. Sotheby's (2011): "From about 1660 onwards, Van der Neer began to focus primarily on winter landscapes". The late pictures are winter scenes with kolf players, snowstorms, wood-collectors, horse-drawn sleighs -- and the moonlight returns only as a modifier, a frozen canal by moonlight (B16, late 1660s). The subject never changes again after 1660; what changes is scale and weather.

What changed -- scale. Early and mid-1640s works are cabinet pictures: W8 at 19.7 x 28.3 cm, W7 at 30.3 x 48.4 cm, W6 at 55 x 70.8 cm, W10 at 57.5 x 89.9 cm. The moonlights grow through the 1640s as the formula proves itself: W9 at 55 x 103 cm, W11 at 78.4 x 110.2 cm, and the dated 1647 moonlight B7 at 83.8 x 114.9 cm. The late change is a step-change, not a drift: the 1660s canvases are 97 x 109 cm (W20, c. 1670) and 133.5 x 167.5 cm (W19, 1660s) -- the latter more than five times the area of the c. 1665 cabinet winter W18 (26.4 x 40.5 cm), painted in the same decade. The Stadel biography states the implication plainly: "Large-format works suggest patrons, but these have not been documented." Small and large run in parallel in the 1660s (W18 and W19, B19 and W20); the career does not move from small to large, it adds large to small. Compare the son: Eglon's pictures stay cabinet-sized for forty years, with only group portraits breaking 70 cm. The father's late scale is the one thing the son never inherited.

What changed -- support and finish. Early/mid: oil on panel almost exclusively -- oak for the winters (W6, two butt-joined oak panels; W15, oak with an old cradle), walnut for the great moonlight (W11, a single walnut panel cut across the whole trunk width), wood "probably oak" for the small National Gallery moonlights (W7, W8, W18). Canvas enters for the large works: W13 (79 x 65.1 cm, c. 1650), W19 (133.5 x 167.5 cm, 1660s), W20 (97 x 109 cm, c. 1670), W16 (60.8 x 75.7 cm, snowstorm, c. 1655-60), B8 (87.6 x 110.5 cm, late 1640s). The rule is mechanical, not stylistic: panel while the picture is under about 90 cm wide, canvas once it passes a metre. The finish is thin throughout -- the NGA technical entries for W6 and W11 agree: thin layers, wet-into-wet, a double ground (off-white under a tan layer for the winter; thin white ground under a thick fawn imprimatura for the moonlight), the ground itself doing the work as a mid-tone. The two signature manual tricks are both ground-revealing: scratching into wet paint with an instrument to let the light imprimatura show through (W11), and brushing a thin pale blue-gray across the tan layer for the translucency of ice (W6). No impasto except selective white touches for untrodden snow.
