The late large canvases are "peculiarly broad and vigorously painted with a Rembrandtesque brush" (Bredius on W20) -- the one place the finish loosens, and it loosens because the picture is a metre wide, not because the painter changed his mind.

What changed -- palette and handling. Early winters are Avercamp's world: high viewpoint, many figures, a bright cold day. The 1643-46 invention replaces brightness with translucence: "his peculiar skill is shown in realizing transparence which allows objects -- even distant -- to appear in the darkness with varieties of warm brown and steel greys" (Britannica). The palette narrows to warm browns and steel grays, with the reddish ocher imprimatura glowing through thin blue-gray paint in the skies (W11) and the tan ground serving as the base colour of the ice (W6). The moonlight pictures add one cold accent -- the moon's track on the water -- and the fire pictures add one warm accent -- the orange forge (W14), the burning village (B9). The late winters widen the palette again: blue and pink skies lit by the setting sun, "a range of cumulus clouds boiling" (Sotheby's on Frozen River at Sunset), russet clouds behind a town at evening (W18), the tinted light of the setting sun refracted through cloud and moist atmosphere (B17's Norton Simon note). And the one handling change the sources name as a gift: "he always avoids the impression of frostiness" (Britannica) -- the ice is painted warm, yellow-brown and peaty (B21), the snow is painted as light on a tan ground, and the cold is conveyed by the behaviour of the light, never by a cold palette.

What stayed constant. (1) The river running into the distance between dark banks, with the light source -- moon, sun, fire -- placed low and central, so the water carries the light back to the viewer. Academia Aesthetics: "a river runs into the distance between dark banks of trees and houses, and the moon, often half-hidden by cloud, sets the edges of the clouds and the water alight." (2) The invented place. The NGA on W11 and the National Gallery on W8 agree: the settings are composites and inventions, not topographical records; real landmarks (Kostverloren, the Amstel town gate, Maarssen) appear as occasional compositional aids. (3) The small figure as scale and life, never as subject: a family crossing a bridge with a dog, a couple in the trees, a farrier at a forge, kolf players, skaters -- all staffage that gives the light something to fall on. (4) The monogram: AV DN in ligature, coupled with a date on the earliest pictures (1639, 1642, 1645, 1647) and then carried without dates -- which is why the chronology after 1646 rests on style, costume and the catalogues raisonnes, and why so many datings in this leg are ranges with named disagreements. (5) The two-subject career: moonlight and winter, in roughly equal numbers -- "his daylight icescapes with golfers, sleighers, and fishermen are about as numerous as his moonlight scenes" (Britannica) -- with the balance tipping to winter only after 1660.

## Disputes and unverified items (flagged)

1. Birthplace: Gorinchem vs Amsterdam. (MOST IMPORTANT FLAG, with 8.) GORINCHEM: Wikipedia (2008 Rene van Dijk research "has established that Van Der Neer was born in Gorinchem"); Lempertz ("It was only in 2008 that researchers were able to determine the birthplace"); Richard Green ("born in 1604 at Gorinchem"); V&A ("spent his youth in Arkel near Gorinchem"); Academia ("Born in Gorinchem (Gorkum) around 1603-04"). AMSTERDAM: NGA/Wheelock ("probably born in Amsterdam"); Stadel ("born in Amsterdam, probably around 1603/04"). The 2008 archival research is the newer evidence and Wikipedia/Lempertz treat it as settled; the NGA (Wheelock, updated 2018) still says "probably born in Amsterdam." The study gives Gorinchem with the Amsterdam tradition footnoted, and presents neither as certain. Note the Stadel's internal tension: it says he was born in Amsterdam AND that he married "in his hometown" in 1629, while the NGA says the marriage was in Amsterdam's Nieuwe Kerk -- consistent with each other only if Amsterdam is the birthplace.
2. Birth year: c. 1603 vs 1603/04 vs 1604. c. 1603: Wikipedia ("c. 1603"). 1603/04: NGA, Stadel, V&A, Academia, Sotheby's headers ("Gorinchem 1603-1677 Amsterdam" is a range, not a year). 1604: Richard Green ("born in 1604"); ArtsDot / WahooArt ("1604-1677"). "December 1603 or early 1604": Watch Artworks / NiceArtGallery (secondary). Working rule: give c. 1603/04 and footnote the rest; never give a single year as certain.
3. The 1642 document: age "roughly thirty-four" implies born c. 1608. Stadel: "A document dated 12 May 1642 gives his age as 'roughly thirty-four'." A man born in late 1603 would be 38 in May 1642. Either "roughly" is doing heavy work, or the document's age is approximate in the loose 17th-century sense, or the 1603/04 dating is wrong. No source read addresses the discrepancy. Record it; do not resolve it. (Compare the Eglon leg: Houbraken's 1643 birth year for Eglon was a misprint for 1634 -- this family has a history of unreliable ages in documents.)
4. Children: six named vs seven. Six named: Wikipedia (Grietje 1629, Eglon ~1635, Cornelia 1642, Elisabeth 1645, Pieter 1648, Alida 1650); NGA ("The couple's six children were all born in Amsterdam"). Seven: Stadel ("The couple's seven children were born in Amsterdam"). The seventh is presumably Johannes (Jan) van der Neer (1637/8-1665), named by Richard Green as a son who "followed his father as a landscape painter" and by Bonhams as the son with whom Aert kept the tavern -- but Johannes appears in NONE of Wikipedia's six names. Either Wikipedia's list omits Johannes, or the seven-count double-counts. Treat "six children" as the documented list and Johannes as an additional son attested by Richard Green/Bonhams/Stadel's count.
5. Eglon birth year in Aert-side sources: 1634 vs ~1635 vs 1635/36. NGA biography (docslib mirror): "Eglon van der Neer (1634-1703)"; Richard Green: "Eglon van der Neer (1634-1703)". Wikipedia (Aert article): Eglon "(~1635)". The #550 leg established 1635/36 (Schavemaker) with a 1634 older tradition. The 1634 figure here is the same older tradition, now confirmed as the one the NGA and Richard Green still print. The Aert study uses 1635/36 for Eglon (per #550) and footnotes 1634.
6. Move to Amsterdam: by 1628 vs by 1630 vs c. 1632. Richard Green: "by 1628 had settled in Amsterdam." National Gallery: "By 1630 he had moved to Amsterdam." V&A / Academia: "moved to Amsterdam in about 1632." These may all be true of different things (settled vs moved vs devoted himself to painting), but no source says so explicitly. Give as "by the early 1630s" with the three datings footnoted.
7. Marriage details: place and wife's origin. NGA: married in Amsterdam's Nieuwe Kerk, 16 March 1629. Stadel: married "in his hometown" 16 March 1629 (same date, compatible only if the hometown is Amsterdam -- see 1). Richard Green: wife "Lijsbeth Govers from Bergen-op-Zoom" -- Bergen-op-Zoom as her origin sits uneasily with her being the sister of the Amsterdam-based Camphuysen brothers, but families move; no source resolves it. Name spellings: Lysbeth / Lijsbeth / Lysets; Govaerts / Govers / Goverts / Govaertsdr. Use "Lysbeth Goverts Camphuysen" (Wikipedia form) and note variants.
8. Five shillings vs five guilders. (MOST IMPORTANT FLAG.) "Five shillings apiece": Britannica 1911 ("his art was so little esteemed that the pictures left by him were valued at about five shillings apiece"), repeated by Wikipedia, Lempertz ("the paintings in his estate were only worth around five shillings each") and the Britannica-derived mirrors. "Five guilders or less": NGA/Wheelock, from the bankruptcy inventory itself -- "The inventory of his possessions made at the time indicates that most of his paintings were appraised at the low price of five guilders or less." Five guilders is a Dutch document figure; five shillings is the English translation tradition (a guilder was roughly two shillings in the 17th century, so the figures are not even equivalent -- five shillings is about half of five guilders). The study gives the NGA's document-based "five guilders or less" as the primary figure and footnotes the famous "five shillings" as the Britannica tradition. The #550 top-pick paragraph's "five shillings apiece" framing is the traditional form, not the documented one (see C613).
9. The town-hall fire pictures: documentary vs inspired. Britannica via Wikipedia: he "made this accident the subject for two or three pictures, now in the galleries of Berlin and Copenhagen." Sotheby's 2011 lot note: "although van der Neer is not known to have created a documentary work himself, the dramatic blaze may well have inspired the depictions of fiery landscapes within his oeuvre." The Berlin picture is a moonlit landscape with a burning village (Schulz no. 1357); the Copenhagen picture is *Fire in Amsterdam by Night* (Statens Museum for Kunst, oil on canvas 58.8 x 71.7 cm). Use Sotheby's correction: he painted fires; he is not documented as painting THE fire.
10. First dated works and the Brookeriana date. (a) First dated painting: genre scene 1632 (NGA) vs "first known work dates from 1632" (Richard Green) -- these agree. (b) First dated landscape: 1633 (Sotheby's) vs the earliest monogrammed-and-dated pictures being the 1639 Rijksmuseum winter landscape and the 1642 Kiel winter landscape (Britannica) -- the difference is monogram + date vs date alone. (c) First dated winter landscape: 1642 (NGA) vs "begun painting winter scenes by 1641" (Thyssen) vs 1639 (Britannica's Rijksmuseum winter landscape). (d) Brookeriana *Moonlit landscape*: signed and dated AV DN 1647 (Sotheby's) vs Schulz no. 1188 "as dated 1645." Give each work its own dating with its own source; do not harmonise.
11. Teacher: Rafael Camphuysen, both brothers, or none -- and the RKD's Jacob Gerritsz. Cuyp. Bonhams: Rafael "the former probably being his master." Stadel: "only collaboration with his brother Joachim Govertsz. Camphuysen is documented." NGA: "it seems that he developed his style rather independently" and "That Camphuysen's early influence encouraged Van der Neer to develop this genre of painting, however, seems unlikely" (on the nocturne specifically). RKD (via Wikipedia and Google Arts and Culture): Jacob Gerritsz. Cuyp was Aert's teacher -- and is listed as teacher of BOTH Rafael Camphuysen and Aert, so the two teacher-claims may both be true in sequence (Dordrecht, then Amsterdam), or one may be a list error; unresolved in this leg. The study must present the teacher question as unresolved, not as "taught by Rafael Camphuysen."
12. Last work: 1665 vs c. 1670 vs 1677. V&A: "his earliest work seems to date from 1642, and the latest from 1665." Richard Green: "He continued to paint, probably until the beginning of the 1670s." Schulz dates the Bonhams winter landscape (W20) to c. 1670 and calls it the apogee. Meisterdrucke lists a River landscape by Moonlight dated 1677 (B22). The V&A's 1665 is a latest-DATED-work claim; Richard Green's is a latest-activity estimate. The 1677 date is a single listing with no scholarly corroboration in the material read. Give as "latest dated work 1665; painting probably continued into the early 1670s; Schulz dates W20 to c. 1670."
13. Name spelling. "Aert van der Neer" (standard) vs "Aernout" / "Artus" / "Aart" / "Arent" / "Arnould" (Wikipedia, Lempertz, Illustrated Magazine of Art 1853). The monogram is AV DN in ligature, "A.V. and D.N. interlaced" (Britannica). Use "Aert" for the study title and note the variants.
14. Burial and wife's death: single-source. "He was buried in the Nieuwe Zijds Kapel. His wife, Lysbeth, had predeceased him." -- NiceArtGallery only, in leg 1's reading (a 2025 secondary site). No museum or scholarly source read confirms either fact. Flag as unverified; do not state as fact in the study without a second source.
15. "Came to winter scenes quite late" (National Gallery NG1288). The NG1288 page says: "Starting as a landscape painter, van der Neer came to winter scenes quite late in his career." Against dated winter landscapes from 1641/42 -- i.e. the first decade of a career that began c. 1632. The NG wording is loose (perhaps meaning late relative to Avercamp, or late relative to the tonal landscapes); treat as imprecise catalogue prose, not a chronology claim.
16. River View by Moonlight (W9) dating: c. 1646 vs 1645 vs c. 1650-1655. Richard Green (dealer scholarship, citing the Six Collection): c. 1646, "first really remarkable". Google Arts and Culture (Rijksmuseum data): ca. 1650 - ca. 1655. WahooArt and related listings: 1645. The spread is ten years for one picture; the 1646 dating is the best-attested (a named provenance and a named scholar), but the Rijksmuseum's own 1650-55 range cannot be dismissed. Treat as unresolved.
17. Mills winter landscape (B21) dating: c. 1643-4 vs c. 1645 vs 1655-60. Schulz: c. 1643-4. Van Suchtelen: c. 1645. Broos (1990, first discussion): 1655-1660. Sutton compared it to the Corcoran 1645 picture (W6) and "excluded a later dating in the 1650s". Three datings spanning seventeen years for a picture unknown to scholars before 1975. The early dating (Schulz/van Suchtelen) is the current scholarly preference; Broos's 1655-60 is on record.
18. Snowstorm picture (W16) dating: c. 1655-60 vs "around 1660?". Sutton and van Suchtelen: c. 1655-60, from the costumes. Schulz: "around 1660?". The question mark is Schulz's own; the two are barely a disagreement, but the costume dating is the stated basis and should be cited as such.
19. Copies, forgeries and fake monograms. The V&A on B23: demand in 1770-80 and 1815-40 "led to a series of copies and forgeries often inscribed with fake monograms", and Schulz (2002) rates the V&A picture "questionable". The Rijksmuseum holds a River View by Moonlight "manner of" Aert van der Neer, c. 1850-1875, 70 x 104 cm, "in the past attributed to Aert van der Neer, probably because it is monogrammed 'AV'" (Rijksmuseum, via search). The Wallace Collection holds two more later items: "Scene on a Canal, c. 1680-1780 (after)" and "A River Scene by Moonlight, probably 18th C (style of)". The monogram AV DN is not proof of authorship on its own; it was forged for a century after his death. The Stadel biography notes copies after his works already existed in 1664 -- in his own lifetime.
20. Cuyp collaboration: who painted what. The joint works are traditional attributions, not documented collaborations. Britannica: in the Louvre sunset "Cuyp evidently painted the foreground and cows"; in the National Gallery picture "Cuyp signs his name on the pail of a milkmaid"; in the Staedel picture the fishermen, dog and sportsman "are Cuyp's". "Evidently" is doing the work in all three. The National Gallery milkmaid picture is not identified by inventory number in the material read and may or may not be W13. Do not assign hands within a picture beyond what the sources state, and do not treat the Cuyp signature on the pail as proof Cuyp painted the whole foreground.
21. Moonlit Landscape with Bridge dimensions: 78.4 x 110.2 cm vs 102 x 133 cm. NGA: overall 78.4 x 110.2 cm; framed 101.6 x 132.7 cm. WahooArt: "It measures 102 x 133 cm" -- the framed size, presented as the picture's size. Cite 78.4 x 110.2 cm; never cite 102 x 133 cm as the panel.
22. River View in Winter SK-C-191 (B18) dimensions: 64 x 79 cm vs 64 x 55.5 cm. The Commons file record (citing the Rijksmuseum) gives height 64 cm, width 79 cm. The FeelTheArt listing gives 0.64 m x 0.555 m. The 79 cm width matches the picture's wide format and the Commons record; the 55.5 cm figure is likely a transposed or erroneous listing, and FeelTheArt's descriptions are AI-generated. Cite 64 x 79 cm as reported, and never cite 64 x 55.5 cm.
23. A Frozen River by a Town at Evening (W18) medium: wood vs canvas. National Gallery: oil on wood (probably oak). FeelTheArt (AI-generated): "This oil on canvas artwork". The museum record wins; the canvas claim is an AI-generated error and should not be repeated.
24. Sports on a Frozen River dating: c. 1648 vs c. 1660. NiceArtGallery lists "Sports on a Frozen River (c. 1648)" as a prime example of the winter work; the Met listing (WahooArt/ArtsDot) dates its Sports on a Frozen River to 1660. These may be two different pictures sharing a title, or one picture with two datings -- the material read does not settle it. The Met picture (B13) is cited as 1660 on the museum-derived listings; the c. 1648 figure is from a secondary essay. Flagged.
25. An Evening View near a Village dimensions: 162.5 x 121.5 cm, untrusted. The only source for this figure is a FeelTheArt page whose text is labelled AI-generated. No museum listing in the material read gives dimensions for this National Gallery picture. The figure would make it the second-largest van der Neer in the material read, which is exactly why it is not cited as fact here. It is listed in "Further names" only, with the flag.
26. Winter in Holland: Skating Scene location confusion. Older secondary sources and the paintingsbefore1800 visit notes place it at the National Gallery of Art, Washington -- correct. The Detroit Institute of Arts appears in this leg only as the lender venue for a different picture (B20, lent by D. Katz, exhibited Detroit). Do not move W6 to Detroit; do not move B20 to Washington.
27. Hofstede de Groot no. 10 and topographical exactitude. Hofstede de Groot identified the Sotheby's 2012 Amstel picture (B20) as a specific Amsterdam view with the old town gate. Sotheby's (2012) replies that "such topographical exactitude would, however, be unusual for Van der Neer, whose primary concern... was to capture the effects of light and atmosphere". The identification is Hofstede de Groot's, not a consensus, and the picture's value in the literature is as a winter scene, not a view.
28. Jacob Gerritsz. Cuyp death: 1651 vs 1651/52. Wikipedia infobox 1652; Aelbert article 1651/52. Unresolved in this leg.
29. Benjamin Cuyp family: Houbraken vs RKD. Houbraken says he was a pupil of his uncle Jacob, taught together with his cousin Aelbert; RKD says Benjamin and Jacob were both sons of the glasspainter Gerrit Gerritsz. Cuyp, Benjamin learned from his older half-brother Jacob, and he was therefore Aelbert's UNCLE, not cousin. He influenced Barent van Calraet and was followed by Maerten Fransz van der Hulst.
30. Berchem birth: 1620 vs 1621/2. Baptised 1 October 1620 (Wikipedia) vs 1621/2 (National Galleries of Scotland).
31. Avercamp death: 1634 vs 1635. Buried Kampen 15 May 1634 (burial record) vs "1585/1586-1635" in one docslib snippet (error; prefer the burial date).
32. Isack van Ostade spelling and death: Isaac/Isack/Izaak -- "Isaac van Ostade" has 0 log mentions, "Isack van Ostade" has 4 (5009, 5012, 5013, 5017); same person. NGS gives death "after 1649" vs the burial record 16 October 1649.
33. Wynants birth: c. 1631/32 vs c. 1635. c. 1631/32 (Eisele/NiceArtGallery) vs c. 1635 (English Heritage/Wikidata).
34. Asselijn teacher and birthplace: Esaias van de Velde (Wikipedia) vs Jan Martszen the Younger (NGA); birth Dieppe vs Diemen (old revision).
35. Hackaert death: 1685 vs in or after 1685 (Dayton).
36. Dujardin birth: c. 1622 (old estimate, Kilian 2005, from a 1672 document giving his age as about 50) vs the baptism record 27 September 1626.
37. Pynacker birth: 1620(?) (Sotheby's lot headings; veryimportantlot "ROTTERDAM ?1620") vs 15 February 1622 (Wikipedia).
38. Wouwerman cross-influence claim rests on watchartworks (low-quality source); suggestive only. "Aert van der Neer occasionally painted winter landscapes that included horses in the manner of Philips Wouwerman" -- plausible but not evidenced.
39. Aernout van der Neer: no independent dates; record is two Sotheby's hedges ("thought to be", "likely trained"). A study now would be invention.
40. Van Goyen debt vs assets: Wikipedia's 18,000-guilders debt vs Britannica's "possessed of land and houses to the amount of 15,000 florins" -- debt and assets are different figures, not a direct contradiction, but the study should not quote one without the other.
41. Koninck birth, Dou spelling, Beerstraten baptism. Philips Koninck birth 5 November vs 15 November 1619 (Stadel). Gerrit Dou vs Gerard Dou -- same person, two spellings in the log. Jan Abrahamsz. Beerstraten baptism 31 May vs 31 March vs 1 March 1622 (unresolved, carried from #538).

## Dimensions verified vs not verified (tally)

Under the definition established in #533 (museum or collection page read end to end):
- VERIFIED: 3 (W10 Moonlit Landscape with a View of the New Amstel River and Castle Kostverloren, 57.5 x 89.9 cm, EN Wikipedia read end to end; W11 Moonlit Landscape with Bridge, 78.4 x 110.2 cm, EN Wikipedia read end to end; W14 The Farrier, 48.3 x 61.3 cm, Met object page read end to end).
- REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED: 11 (W6, W7, W8, W9, W13, W15, W16, W17, W18, W19, W20). W6's figure comes from the NGA object record via search result and the Docslib full text; the NGA scholarly article read end to end gives no dimensions in its extracted text. W11's dimensions are confirmed by the NGA object record via search result as well as by the Wikipedia page read end to end.
- NOT VERIFIED: 6 (W1, W2, W3, W4, W5, W12 -- no reliable dimension figure found in the material read).
- Total: 20.
- Beyond the 20, no further dimension is VERIFIED on the same definition in this run; the B-entries' figures rest on search results and secondary listings throughout.

## Etchings / prints / drawings

No etchings, prints or drawings by Aert van der Neer are recorded in the material read. No etching by him is named in any source read; no drawing is documented; the Schulz catalogue raisonne was not read, so a print or drawing oeuvre cannot be ruled out from first principles -- but nothing in the three legs evidences one, and the study must not invent it. The print line in this study runs the other way: Hendrick Goudt's engravings after Adam Elsheimer (notably the Flight into Egypt, 1613) carried the Roman nocturne into the Dutch print market a generation before Aert's first moonlight of 1644, and the V&A traces the Amsterdam/Haarlem nocturne to "Caravaggio and Elsheimer via Goudt". What circulated in print after Aert were copies and forgeries, often inscribed with fake monograms (see Disputes 19), not etchings by his own hand. Do not invent a print oeuvre for him.

## Aesthetic logic

Van der Neer's aesthetic is the aesthetic of the single lamp in a large dark. Everything he does is a variation on one system: a low light source -- moon, sun, fire -- placed against a wide sky and a receding river, with the ground doing the glowing and the figures kept small enough to measure the space. The mechanism is never invention of a new subject; it is always the same two subjects -- moonlight and winter -- re-lit, re-composed, re-weathered, and the re-lighting is the whole claim to value. That is why the sources agree on the paradox at the centre of the career: the pictures are patient, thin, jewel-finished and numerous, while the life is a bankrupt innkeeper's; the economics are invisible in the work and total in the life. The pictures are small because the light effects demand closeness -- the scratches, the stipple, the wood grain showing through only resolve at close range; they are dark because the light is the subject and the darkness is its stage; they are still because the moon is a brief event and the ice is a held moment. The late turn to large canvas is the same logic carried outward: the river gets wider, the sky gets taller, the figures stay small, and the finish loosens only because the picture is a metre wide, not because the painter changed his mind. The one pocket of direct looking in the whole oeuvre is the composite place itself -- houses from Amsterdam, a church from a small town, a castle ruin from the Amstel -- assembled in the studio so the light can be consistent, because a real place lit badly would be a worse picture than an invented place lit truly. Everything else is a copy of an evening, and the copies are the point: a painter who painted the same river a hundred times, and made each evening a different mood.

## Positions

- **P1 -- Independence: the style was developed rather independently.** NGA: "because Van der Neer's scenes appear to be based on excursions he made in and around Amsterdam, it seems that he developed his style rather independently" -- and "Virtually nothing is known of Van der Neer's artistic contacts during the 1630s and 1640s." Position (by implication, via Wheelock): the nocturne was not taught to him; it was worked out alone, on walks around Amsterdam. (Attribution: Wheelock 2018.)
- **P2 -- Mood over topography.** NGA: "In conceiving this image, Van der Neer was more interested in creating the mood of nature than in recording an actual setting." Position: the picture's truth is atmospheric, not topographical; a composite place lit consistently is truer than a real place lit badly. (Attribution: Wheelock 2018.)
- **P3 -- Coloured light as the subject.** V&A: "*Coloured light* is thus typically considered the essential characteristic of Aert van der Neer's style." Position: light is not the illumination of a subject; light IS the subject, and the river, the town, the skaters are its occasions. (Attribution: V&A catalogue text.)
- **P4 -- The late bloomer's reversion.** Sotheby's (Frozen River note) and Thyssen: after the tonal landscapes of his predecessors, Van der Neer "returned instead to the broader views and compositional motifs that Avercamp had made famous earlier in the century" -- and "reverted to the time-honoured, encyclopaedic winter scenes." Position (by implication): the older, additive formula was not superseded; it was waiting for someone to put new light into it. (Attribution: Sotheby's/Schulz; Sutton/Thyssen.)
- **P5 -- Persistence through poverty.** Bonhams: "It is a testament to his dedication as an artist, however, that van der Neer continued to paint while in a state of extreme poverty on the Kerkestraat in Amsterdam until his death." Position (by implication): the work continues regardless of the market; the tavern and the bankruptcy are interruptions, not endings. (Attribution: Bonhams lot essay.)
- **P6 -- The scholar's verdict: unrivalled in moonlight.** Richard Green: he "is unrivalled as a painter of moonlight in Dutch art." Bonhams: "As a painter of twilight, moonlit and winter landscapes, Aert van der Neer was one of the most important landscapists of his age, celebrated for his subtle portrayal of atmosphere." Sotheby's (kolf note): "In Aert van der Neer the winter landscape... reached its apogee." (Attribution: Richard Green; Bonhams; Sotheby's.)
- **P7 -- The contemporary market's counter-verdict.** V&A: "his work was much imitated during his lifetime, but taste changed in the later 1660s, and he ended his days in poverty." Britannica on the earliest dated pictures: "immature works both, of poor quality." The 19th-century verdict (Britannica) and the market verdict (five shillings / five guilders) stand against the modern one; the study must carry both. Recorded here so the study does not inherit only the flattering half of the record.
- **P8 -- Houbraken's amateur framing, corrected by the marriage licence.** Houbraken (via Wikipedia/secondary): Aert worked as steward to the lords of Arkel, and "became an amateur painter possibly upon contact with the Amsterdam painters Rafael and Joachim Govertsz Camphuysen, whose sister Lysbeth he married in 1629." The Stadel marriage-licence evidence (identified as a painter in 1629, training already complete) contradicts the "amateur" framing; the study should say so. (Attribution: Houbraken via secondary quotation; Stadel.)
- **P9 -- The heyday of the Dutch nocturne.** Northern Nocturnes exhibition (HNA review, via searches): "With the exception of Aert van der Neer and Egbert van der Poel, few artists turned their attention to the night scene with any regularity"; one exhibition room is "Aert van der Neer, the heyday of the Dutch nocturne (c. 1645-60)". The V&A (via searches): "During the first half of the 1640s, Aert van der Neer and colleagues in Amsterdam and Haarlem developed a new genre of nocturne landscapes." Position (by implication, via the exhibition and the V&A): the nocturne was a collective invention of the early 1640s, and Aert is its heyday, not its sole inventor. (Attribution: Northern Nocturnes/HNA; V&A.)

## Quotes

- **Q1 -- Britannica 1911 on translucence.** "He is known for his skill in rendering translucence, which allows objects even at distance to appear in the darkness with varieties of warm brown and steel greys." Source: Encyclopaedia Britannica 11th ed., "Neer, van der," as incorporated in EN Wikipedia, read end to end. Speaker: Britannica ABOUT him.
- **Q2 -- Britannica 1911 on frostiness.** "But he always avoids the impression of frostiness, which is one of his great gifts." Source: same. Speaker: Britannica ABOUT him.
- **Q3 -- NGA on mood over setting.** "In conceiving this image, Van der Neer was more interested in creating the mood of nature than in recording an actual setting." Source: NGA, Dutch Paintings of the Seventeenth Century, *Moonlit Landscape with Bridge*, read end to end. Speaker: the NGA (Wheelock) ABOUT him.
- **Q4 -- NGA technical entry on the imprimatura.** "He suggested the translucency of the clouds, for example, by allowing the reddish ocher imprimatura covering the walnut panel to remain visible through thinly applied bluish gray paint." Source: same NGA article, technical entry. Speaker: the NGA ABOUT his technique.
- **Q5 -- Sotheby's on the leaden ice.** "Although the sky is blue and pink, lit by the setting sun, a range of cumulus clouds boiling on the horizon, these colors are barely reflected by the icy surface of the river. It is leaden in color, touched with shades of yellow and green, and chills us to look at." Source: Sotheby's, *Frozen River at Sunset*, lot 35, 2015, read end to end. Speaker: Sotheby's ABOUT the picture.
- **Q6 -- V&A on coloured light.** "Coloured light is thus typically considered the essential characteristic of Aert van der Neer's style." Source: V&A, *Moonlight and River Scene*, historical context, read end to end. Speaker: the V&A ABOUT him.
- **Q7 -- Sotheby's on the winter landscape's apogee.** "In Aert van der Neer the winter landscape, pioneered by Hendrick Avercamp, and essayed by most of the leading landscape painters of the Dutch Golden Age, reached its apogee, so that when we think of a Dutch village in winter, Van der Neer's paintings of them are the most likely to spring first to mind." Source: Sotheby's, *Winter landscape with kolf players*, lot 38, 2018, catalogue note (search result; page not opened end to end). Speaker: Sotheby's ABOUT him.
- **Q8 -- Richard Green on moonlight.** "He excelled in extraordinary poetic landscapes of sunrise and sunset and is unrivalled as a painter of moonlight in Dutch art." Source: Richard Green gallery, artist page, read end to end. Speaker: Richard Green ABOUT him.
- **Q9 -- NGA on the composite setting.** "The setting, thus, is a composite of various aspects of city and country life that Van der Neer has brought into a harmonious whole." Source: NGA, *Moonlit Landscape with Bridge*, read end to end. Speaker: the NGA ABOUT the picture.
- **Q10 -- V&A on the nocturne's invention.** "During the first half of the 1640s, Aert van der Neer and colleagues in Amsterdam and Haarlem developed a new genre of nocturne landscapes." Source: V&A, via search results in leg 3 (page not opened end to end). Speaker: the V&A ABOUT the genre.
- **Q11 -- Northern Nocturnes on the two-man night.** "With the exception of Aert van der Neer and Egbert van der Poel, few artists turned their attention to the night scene with any regularity." Source: Northern Nocturnes exhibition, HNA review, via search results in leg 3 (page not opened end to end). Speaker: the exhibition ABOUT the Dutch nocturne.
- **Q12 -- Bonhams on the impoverished artist.** "Aert van der Neer's ultimate story is that of the classic impoverished artist, unappreciated in his own lifetime." Source: Bonhams lot essay, read end to end in leg 1 (313 lines, of which roughly 213 are site chrome). Speaker: Bonhams ABOUT him.
- **Q13 -- Richard Green on his standing.** "Aert van der Neer was one of the most important European landscape painters of the seventeenth century." Source: Richard Green gallery, artist page, read end to end. Speaker: Richard Green ABOUT him.
- **No Q from Van der Neer himself, and no verbatim Houbraken sentence retrieved.** No surviving letter, treatise, or recorded remark by Aert van der Neer was found in any source read, and no verbatim Houbraken sentence about Aert was retrieved in any leg (the steward claim reaches us only as paraphrase in Wikipedia, Lempertz, the National Gallery and the NGA, all citing Houbraken *Groote Schouburgh* 3:172). Any "quote" attributed to him directly would be invented. The study must not put words in his mouth.

The transmitter (1): Hendrick Goudt (c. 1583 - 1648), Utrecht engraver. ## Future candidates

Top pick for #552 is Adam Elsheimer (1578-1610), the German in Rome who taught the Dutch to paint by moonlight. His Flight into Egypt (c. 1609, Munich) is the first naturalistic night sky in paint, Milky Way included; Hendrick Goudt engraved seven of his paintings, and the Flight engraving of 1613 carried the nocturne into the Dutch print market a generation before Aert's first moonlight of 1644. The link is print-mediated, NOT contact: Elsheimer died in Rome in December 1610, seven years before Aert is first recorded as a painter (identified as a painter in his 1629 marriage licence) and thirty-four years before Aert's first Moonlight Scene of 1644. Aelbert Cuyp (20 October 1620 - 15 November 1691; dates search-verified only) -- main 1: the friend who painted the daylight into Aert's moonlight. Shared canvases: in the Louvre Landscape at Sunset Cuyp "evidently painted the foreground and cows" (Britannica); in a National Gallery picture he signs his name on the pail of a milkmaid whose figure and red skirt he painted; in the Staedel picture the fishermen, dog and sportsman "are Cuyp's". The golden light against Aert's silver one -- the daylight pair to the nocturne, and the study the series needs next. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; 3 whole-log mentions (5002, 5009, 5127), all carries; no local file; muse slug study/aelbert-cuyp.md 404, re-checked by curl this run. Promoted out of the carried #534 block, which now carries 9 names.

Jan van Goyen (13 January 1596 - 27 April 1656; dates search-verified only) -- main 2: the tonal root Aert's moonlight inverts. Van Goyen, Salomon van Ruysdael and Pieter de Molijn founded the tonal landscape of the 1630s -- thin paint, low horizon, a near-monochrome palette -- and Aert's coloured-light nocturne is the same thin paint turned to night. The bankruptcy parallel is exact: van Goyen died 18,000 guilders in debt after speculating in tulips and property; Aert went bankrupt in 1662 and died in poverty. The two failures frame the same market. The study would be the teacher whose influence his pupil's work does not show -- the honest negative case. The doorway is the teacher whose pupil shows no trace of him. (The 14 names carried in #547 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Govert Flinck (25 January 1615 - 2 February 1660), Ferdinand Bol (24 June 1616 - 24 August 1680), Willem Drost (baptised 19 April 1633 - buried 25 February 1659) (mains); Jacob Moelaert (1649-1727) (reserve); Margaretha van Godewijk (30 or 31 August 1627 - 2 November 1677), Johannes Vollevens (1649, Geertruidenberg - 1728, The Hague) (alternates); spares: Matthijs Balen (1684-1766), Hendrik Noteman (1654-1737), Augustin Terwesten (1649-1711), Werner Sumowski (8 March 1931 - 3 September 2015), Ernst van de Wetering (9 March 1938 - 11 August 2021), Jacob Adriaensz Backer (1608/9 - 27 August 1651), Barthoud van Slingelandt (1654-1711), Godfrey Kneller (8 August 1646 - 19 October 1723). Govert Flinck (25 January 1615 - 2 February 1660; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 135 lines) -- main 1, strongest NEW name: the Rembrandt pupil who left the manner for Rubens, and whose last commission Rembrandt himself inherited. Apprenticed to a silk mercer, then Lambert Jacobsz in Leeuwarden with Jacob Backer; Sandrart, visiting Holland in 1637, found him acknowledged as one of Rembrandt's best pupils, living in the dealer Hendrick van Uylenburgh's house; he followed Rembrandt's style in all works 1636-1648, then turned to the swelling forms and grand action of Rubens, winning the patronage of Friedrich Wilhelm I, the Great Elector of Brandenburg, and John Maurice of Nassau; Amsterdam burgher 1652, married the heiress Sophie van der Houven 1656, intimate with the poet Joost van den Vondel; in November 1659 the burgomaster contracted him for 12 canvases of David, Samson, Manius Curius Dentatus, Horatius Cocles and the Batavians, and he died leaving only sketches -- Rembrandt was asked to fill one and produced The Conspiracy of Claudius Civilis, which the authorities rejected. His Landscape with Obelisk was historically mistaken for Rembrandt and was stolen in the 1990 Gardner heist. The doorway is the Rembrandt pupil who left the manner for Rubens -- and whose last commission Rembrandt inherited. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions ("Govert Flinck" 0; "Govaert Flinck" 0); no local file; not carried in #522-#546 as a candidate. FLAG: spelling Govert vs Govaert. Ferdinand Bol (24 June 1616 - 24 August 1680; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 93 lines) -- main 2: the Dordrecht Rembrandt pupil who became Amsterdam's most commissioned painter, and whose pictures were mistaken for Rembrandt's for a century. Born in Dordrecht, son of the surgeon Balthasar Bol (painted by Rembrandt twenty years later); apprentice first of Jacob Gerritsz Cuyp in Dordrecht and/or Abraham Bloemaert in Utrecht, then of Rembrandt after 1630, living in his house on the Sint Antoniesbreestraat, neighbours with Flinck; own studio 1641; Amsterdam burgher 1652; married Elisabeth Dell 1653, whose father held Admiralty and wine-merchants' posts that later commissioned him; head of the guild 1655, chimney pieces for Jacob van Campen's new town hall and four more for the Admiralty; by the mid-1650s receiving more official commissions than any other artist in Amsterdam, his palette lightened and his figures grown elegant; four paintings for the Trip brothers' mansions; his pupil was Godfrey Kneller; he retired from painting after remarrying Anna van Erckel in 1669 and died on the Herengracht. His Portrait of Elisabeth Bas and many others were falsely attributed to Rembrandt in the 19th century. The doorway is the Dordrecht boy who out-commissioned every painter in Amsterdam -- and was then mistaken for his master. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#546 as a candidate (mentioned once in the #546 Future candidates section inside the Jacob Gerritsz Cuyp carried entry -- "Teacher of Ferdinand Bol..." -- a mention, not a proposal). FLAG: the 1822 James list gives his death as 1681 (error); apprenticeship dated only as "after 1630". Willem Drost (baptised 19 April 1633 - buried 25 February 1659; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 163 lines) -- main 3: the mysterious pupil who died at 25 in Venice, and whose pictures still carry Rembrandt's name. Rembrandt's student in the late 1640s or early 1650s per Houbraken, developing a close working relationship; his 1654 Bathsheba with King David's Letter answers Rembrandt's Bathsheba of the same year, same subject, different treatment, both now in the Louvre; Amsterdam until 1655, then Rome, friend of Karel Lot (Johann Carl Loth) and Joan vander Meer, collaborating with Loth on a lost Four Evangelists series in Venice, where he died; his Portrait of a Young Woman with her Hands Folded on a Book was attributed to Rembrandt for more than 300 years, and the Polish Rider -- found 1897, Frick Collection -- has been argued as his since Josua Bruyn in 1984, though the Frick has not changed the attribution, and a group of "Rembrandt" drawings reattributed to Drost is more widely accepted. The doorway is the pupil who died at 25 in Venice and left Rembrandt holding his name. Jacob Moelaert (1649-1727; dates from the Leiden Collection de Gelder biography snippet) -- the reserve: the friend who left de Gelder his Rembrandts. Amateur painter and printseller, pupil of Nicolaes Maes in Dordrecht; well acquainted with de Gelder, and at his death in 1727 left de Gelder three albums of prints and drawings, including one with work by Rembrandt; de Gelder died suddenly less than a month later, on 27 August of that same year. He is the reserve because his painted oeuvre is thin -- amateur and dealer first -- though his mechanism (the print album as a bequest, the collection as a friendship) is the most intimate in the network. The doorway is the friend whose albums outlived them both by a month. Margaretha van Godewijk (30 or 31 August 1627, Dordrecht - 2 November 1677, Dordrecht; dates search-verified only) -- alternate 1: the Dordrecht polymath with no surviving works. Poet and painter; her father, a Latin-school teacher, taught her Greek, Latin, Italian, French and English; learned to paint from Nicolaes Maes per Houbraken (the RKD says pupil of Cornelis Bisschop), skilled at landscapes, villas, houses, flowers and ships in oils, watercolours and embroidery, good at astronomy and at diamond-engraving on glass roemers; Houbraken quoted the Dordrecht historian Mathias Balen (1611-1691) on her among his famous women. She is alternate 1 because no works survive -- the study would be built from the life and the loss -- but the doorway (a woman who painted, wrote, and engraved glass in a town that kept Rembrandt's manner) is the sharpest in the alternates. The doorway is the Dordrecht woman who painted, wrote poetry, and engraved glass -- and left nothing to look at. Johannes Vollevens (1649, Geertruidenberg - 1728, The Hague; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 25 lines) -- alternate 2: the portraitist whose birth year Houbraken got wrong by five years. Born in Geertruidenberg, sketched and drew in charcoal so enthusiastically his parents sent him to learn drawing; first lessons from Caspar Netscher per Houbraken, then Nicolaes Maes, then Jan de Baen in The Hague (RKD: Maes's pupil in Dordrecht, moved to The Hague 1664, studied with de Baen); joined the Confrerie Pictura 1674; his son Johannes Vollevens II also became a respected portrait painter; his portrait of Gaspar Fagel survives. He is alternate 2 because his mechanism (the provincial portraitist who kept moving to the next master) is a career shape rather than a system -- but the Houbraken error (born 1654, "the same year that took Fabritius by a stroke of fate", in fact five years earlier) is the chain's own correction made visible. The doorway is the painter Houbraken misdated by the Delft explosion. Werner Sumowski (8 March 1931, Ortelsburg - 3 September 2015, Stuttgart; dates from the HNA obituary snippet) -- spare 4: the scholar who knew the Rembrandt school better than anyone alive. Professor at Stuttgart 1973-1993; from 1979 his ten-volume opus on the drawings of Rembrandt's pupils, and six volumes on the paintings (1983, with later additions) covering 99 named artists and more than 2,500 works; Gary Schwartz's verdict: he knew "more about the Rembrandt school as anyone ever alive". He is a spare, not a main, because he is the cataloguer of the line, not a maker in it -- but the line's attribution problem (de Gelder, Drost, Bol all carried Rembrandt's name) is his life's work. The doorway is the man who sorted ninety-nine pupils from their master. Ernst van de Wetering (9 March 1938 - 11 August 2021; dates from Dictionary of Art Historians and EN Wikipedia snippets) -- spare 5: the Rembrandt police. Joined the Rembrandt Research Project in 1968, chairman from 1993, directed its later stages; doctorate cum laude 1986 on the workshop practice of the early Rembrandt; professor at Amsterdam 1987-1999, Slade Professor at Oxford 2002-03; Rembrandt: the Painter at Work (1997) and Rembrandt: The Painter Thinking (2016); the six-volume A Corpus of Rembrandt Paintings (1982-2014) was the project's principal result; his evaluations were so conclusive on value that he was dubbed "the Rembrandt police", and Gary Schwartz called his work "deleterious to scholarship" because it ended debate on authenticity. The doorway is the chairman who decided which Rembrandts were Rembrandts. Jacob Adriaensz Backer (1608/9, Harlingen - 27 August 1651, Amsterdam; dates from Leiden Collection and EN Wikipedia snippets) -- spare 6: Flinck's bedfellow, and the pupil who may never have been a pupil. Mennonite baker's son; with Flinck a pupil of Lambert Jacobsz in Leeuwarden 1627-1633 -- Houbraken calls Backer Flinck's "bedfellow and companion in Art" -- then Amsterdam, probably working for Hendrick van Uylenburgh; about 140 paintings in twenty years, portraits, religious and mythological subjects, his Company of Cornelis de Graeff schutterstuk hung for years on the same wall as the Night Watch; taught Abraham van den Tempel; died at 42, three months after joining the Remonstrant congregation. The doorway is Flinck's companion, whose Rembrandt apprenticeship may be a three-hundred-year misreading. Godfrey Kneller (8 August 1646, Lubeck - 19 October 1723, London; dates from EN Wikipedia snippet) -- spare 8: Bol's pupil who became England's court painter. Born Gottfried Kniller, son of the Lubeck portrait painter Zacharias Kniller; studied mathematics at Leiden, then became a pupil of Ferdinand Bol and Rembrandt in Amsterdam by his father's choice; earliest dated work, Johann Philipp von Schonborn, 1666; with his brother John Zacharias Kneller to Rome and Venice in the early 1670s, in the studio of Carlo Maratti; to England 1676, patronage of the Duke of Monmouth; the face of Restoration and Augustan England for the next four decades. The doorway is the Lubeck boy Bol taught, who painted every face in England. (The 10 names carried in #546 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Nicolaes Maes (January 1634 - December 1693) (main); Frans van Mieris the Elder (16 April 1635 - 12 March 1681), Caspar Netscher (c. 1639 - 15 January 1684) (alternates); spares: Arnold Boonen (16 December 1669 - 2 October 1729), Antonina Houbraken (30 May 1686 - December 1736), David van Hoogstraten (14 March 1658 - 21 November 1724), Giorgio Vasari (30 July 1511 - 27 June 1574), Cornelis de Bie (10 February 1627 - c. 1712-1715), Filippo Baldinucci (3 June 1625 - 10 January 1696), Willem van Drielenburch (1632 - c. 1687). Nicolaes Maes (January 1634 - December 1693, buried 24 December 1693; dates search-verified only) - main 3: the Dordrecht Rembrandt pupil who broke away and invented the eavesdropper. In Rembrandt's Amsterdam studio c. 1648-1653, back in Dordrecht by December 1653 for twenty years, then Amsterdam from 1673, where he painted some 900 portraits and died rich. His Dordrecht genre pictures (1655-1665) are the hinge of the chain: the eavesdropper at the foot of the stairs, finger to her lips, breaking the fourth wall -- a domestic interior as a listening machine, the exact subject Vermeer would inherit. His registered Dordrecht pupils included Justus de Gelder, Margaretha van Godewijk, Jacob Moelaert and Johannes Vollevens. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he left Dordrecht for Amsterdam portraiture, where de Gelder stayed and kept Rembrandt's manner alive in the town itself. The doorway is the Dordrecht pupil who left Rembrandt, invented the eavesdropper, and then painted nine hundred portraits. FLAG: death 24 December 1693 (burial, Wikipedia) vs 24 November 1693 (Prabook, Alchetron). Frans van Mieris the Elder (16 April 1635 - 12 March 1681; dates search-verified only) - alternate 1: the Leiden fine painter at his smallest and most polished. Goldsmith's son; glass-painter's pupil, then Gerard Dou's "Prince of my students"; panels seldom larger than 12-15 inches; shiny brightness and metallic polish, satin skirts, oyster luncheons and doctor's visits; founder of a three-generation Leiden dynasty (Willem, Frans the Younger). Houbraken is the source for his birth date, and the 1911 Britannica already doubts it. He is alternate 1 because his mechanism (the miniature polish) overlaps van der Werff's, and his life is short on incident -- but he is the fijnschilder standard against which Werff and Schalcken are measured. The doorway is the prince of Dou's students, painting satin on panels the size of a hand. FLAG: birth 16 April 1635 "according to Houbraken" (1911 Britannica: "It is a question whether Houbraken has truly recorded this master's birthday" -- an oyster-luncheon picture is dated 1650); "? 1635" in the 1911 Britannica heading. Caspar Netscher (c. 1639 - 15 January 1684; dates search-verified only) - alternate 2: the Hague portraitist who painted silk. Orphaned young, raised in Arnhem, ter Borch's most gifted pupil in Deventer (c. 1655-1659), then The Hague from 1662, where from the 1670s he was the most sought-after portrait painter in the city; master of oriental rugs, silk and brocade, "introduced an international style to the Northern Netherlands"; painted lying in bed with gout until 15 January 1684, when, "according to Houbraken, 'this affliction affected his bowls, and caused his death'". He is alternate 2 because his mechanism (fabric as the picture) is a single brilliant trick rather than a system -- but it is the trick the whole Hague portrait trade ran on. The doorway is the painter who sold silk by the yard and died painting in bed. Arnold Boonen (16 December 1669 - 2 October 1729; dates search-verified only) - spare 1: the Dordrecht portraitist who carried the candle out of the genre piece and into the state portrait. Pupil first of Arnold Verbuis, then six years (from 1683) under Schalcken; small candlelit genre pictures before 1700, then life-size portraits at the German courts (1694-95) and in Amsterdam from 1696 -- Peter the Great, Marlborough, the Prince and Princess of Orange; teacher of Cornelis Troost. The doorway is the Schalcken pupil who took candlelight to the German courts and came home to paint Peter the Great. Antonina Houbraken (30 May 1686 - December 1736, buried 12 December 1736; dates search-verified only) - spare 3: PROMOTED from the #545 extra verified spares, where she was held back only because no dates were established; this run establishes them (born 30 May 1686, baptised 31 May 1686 -- see C569). The daughter who drew the country. Draughtswoman and portraitist; learned in her father's workshop; assisted father and brother on the Schouburgh designs and provided designs for Pieter Tanje; married the draughtsman Jacobus Stellingwerff 1723 and, after his death in 1727, kept supplying topographical drawings to the collectors Andries and Gerrit Schoemaker until at least 1729 -- hundreds of Dutch sites, towns and buildings drawn on the spot, later embellished with animals and people. The doorway is the daughter who drew the whole country for the collectors' albums. David van Hoogstraten (14 March 1658 - 21 November 1724; dates search-verified only) - spare 4: the poet inside the painter's book. Physician, poet, philologist; Samuel van Hoogstraten's nephew (son of Samuel's brother Frans); prorector of the Amsterdam Latin School; his annotated Latin Phaedrus edition (1701) is his masterpiece, his Dutch verse Phaedrus (1703) a bestseller; his 30-line laudatory poem opens Schouburgh vol. 1; his Groot woordenboek (1725-1733, completed by van Nidek) is the ten-volume dictionary of the Republic of Letters; 61 wedding poems make him one of the most productive occasional poets of the age. The doorway is the poet whose thirty lines open Arnold's book. Giorgio Vasari (30 July 1511 - 27 June 1574; dates search-verified only) - spare 5: the root of the whole genre: painter, architect (the Uffizi, begun 1560; the corridor, 1565) and the inventor of the encyclopedia of artistic biographies -- Le Vite (1550; partly rewritten, enlarged and given woodcut portraits, some imaginary, in 1568), dedicated to Cosimo I de' Medici, with its treatise on technique and its Florentine bias; van Mander and Sandrart both built their Italian sections on him, and Houbraken's book is the third generation of his invention. He is a spare, not a main, because his link to Arnold runs through van Mander and Sandrart -- two removes -- and his mechanism (canon-making by biography) is the series' background, not its next step. The doorway is the man who invented the book Arnold spent his life continuing. Cornelis de Bie (10 February 1627 - c. 1712-1715; dates search-verified only) - spare 6: the Antwerp rhetorician who wrote the South's answer to van Mander. Het Gulden Cabinet (Antwerp, 1662), commissioned by the publisher Jan Meyssens as "the South Netherlandish counterpart of Karel van Mander's Schilder-Boeck": three parts, 95 full-page engraved portraits, biographies and panegyrics of 16th- and 17th-century artists, dedicated to the collector Antoon van Leyen; "the principal source of information for later art historians such as Arnold Houbraken and Jacob Campo Weyerman". A notary and rederijker who deemed it his duty to broadcast the fame of the artists -- the panegyric as a system, against Houbraken's anecdotal biography. The doorway is the Antwerp rhetorician whose Golden Cabinet Houbraken mined. Filippo Baldinucci (3 June 1625 - 10 January 1696; dates search-verified only) - spare 7: the Florentine who corrected Vasari with documents. Cardinal Leopoldo de' Medici's man, put in charge of the Medici drawing collection (the nucleus of the Uffizi's); Notizie de' professori del disegno (1681-1728, six volumes, the last three finished by his son Francesco Saverio), the first universal history of the figurative arts in Europe and "the first to make full use of documents"; the first history of engraving and the first dictionary of artistic terms (both 1686); dozens of Flemish and Dutch painters, including Rembrandt, judged worthy of inclusion. The doorway is the archivist who corrected Vasari with documents -- and wrote the first history of engraving. Willem van Drielenburch (1632 Utrecht - c. 1687 Dordrecht; dates search-verified only) - spare 8: the rampjaar teacher. Bloemaert pupil, landscape painter in the manner of Jan Both; came to Dordrecht 1668; Arnold his pupil in 1672, the year of disaster, when the boy learned less painting than news -- his master "was constantly sending him out to hear the latest news". Also teacher of Wilhelmus Beurs. A small oeuvre and a thinner record than the other spares, but the anecdote is the mechanism: the pupil as messenger, the studio as a newsroom, the year the country nearly ended. The doorway is the teacher who sent his pupil out for the news in the year of disaster. (The 14 names carried in #545 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: George Vertue (1684 - 24 July 1756), Isaak Tirion (1705 in Utrecht - 1765 in Amsterdam), Jan van Gool (1685-1763) (mains); Jan Wagenaar (25 October 1709 - 1 March 1773) (reserve); Thomas Birch (1705-1766), Gerard Edelinck (bapt. 20 October 1640 - 2 April 1707) (alternates); spares: Cornelis Cort (c.1533-1578), Jonas Suyderhoef (1613-1686), Claes Jansz. Visscher (1587 - 19 June 1652), Nicolaes Visscher I (25 January 1618 - buried 11 September 1679), Samuel van Hoogstraten (1627-1678), Jacob Levecq (c.1634-1675), Aert Schouman (4 March 1710 - 5 July 1792), Bartholomeus van der Helst (1613 - buried 16 December 1670). George Vertue (1684 - 24 July 1756; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 123 lines) - the London engraver Houbraken superseded on the *Heads*. Apprenticed at 13, seven years under Michael Vandergucht, pupil of Thomas Gibson at Kneller's academy; Rose and Crown Club with Hogarth and Tillemans; official engraver to the Society of Antiquaries 1717, the only engraver made Fellow; ~500 portraits + ~500 antiquarian plates; *Twelve Heads of Poets* 1730; Rapin's *History of England* for the Knaptons 1736; his *Heads* contribution "submerged by the popularity of Houbraken's 'brilliant but less truthful productions'"; his forty volumes of notebooks, bought by Walpole, became *Anecdotes of Painting in England*. He is main 1 rather than top pick because he is the man Houbraken displaced, not the man Houbraken came from: the London half of the portrait trade, where Arnold is the Amsterdam root. The doorway is the London engraver whose plates Houbraken's brighter ones displaced. The parent confirms the promotion (see C561). Isaak Tirion (1705 in Utrecht - 1765 in Amsterdam; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 21 lines) - the publisher who turned Houbraken's faces into the national history. *Hedendaagsche historie* and *Vaderlandse Historie*, "illustrated with engravings by leading artists of Amsterdam"; moved to Amsterdam by May 1727; 1742 bought Wetstein's Kalverstraat house; *Het Verheerlykt Nederland* 1745-57, continued by his widow 1773-74. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the trade, not the hand: the man who commissioned the faces, where Arnold is the man who wrote the book that needed them. The doorway is the publisher who turned Houbraken's faces into the national history. Jan van Gool (1685-1763; dates search-verified only) - the Hague painter-biographer whose sequel Houbraken illustrated. *Nieuwe Schouburg* (The Hague, 1750), "meant as an update to the original" Schouburgh; regent then director of the Hague Drawing School 1720-1734; Italianate landscapes; his book carries notes on the Hague painters and the founding of the Hague drawing academy. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the sequel, not the source: the man who continued Arnold's book, where Arnold is the man who wrote it. The doorway is the sequel-writer whose new Schouburgh Houbraken illustrated. Jan Wagenaar (25 October 1709 - 1 March 1773; dates search-verified only) - the reserve: the historian whose 21-part national history Houbraken illustrated for Tirion. *Vaderlandsche historie* (1749-1760, 21 vols), continued to 52 vols; also *Tegenwoordige staat* and the Amsterdam history (1760, 3 vols); at first refused to publish his name, revealing himself in vol. 20 (1759). He is the reserve because he is the text, not the hand: the man who wrote the history Houbraken pictured, but whose own drawn work is nil. The doorway is the historian whose 21-part national history Houbraken illustrated for Tirion. Gerard Edelinck (bapt. 20 October 1640 - 2 April 1707; dates search-verified only) - alternate 2: the Paris portrait engraver whose burin Houbraken studied. He is alternate 2 because he is the model, not the line: the standard Houbraken measured himself against, a generation before and a country away. (The 14 names carried in #544 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Francois Morellon la Cave (15 April 1696 - 9 July 1768), Gerard Hoet (22 August 1648 - 2 December 1733), Etienne Picart le Romain (1632-1721) (mains); Prosper Marchand (11 March 1678 - 14 June 1756) (reserve); Johann Philipp Endelich (1700 - buried 18 Sept 1760), Cornelis de Bruyn (1652-1727) (alternates); spares: Charles Le Brun (1619-1690), Benoit Audran the Elder (1661-1721), Sebastien Leclerc (1637-1714), Philipp von Stosch (22 March 1691 - 7 Nov 1757), Levinus Vincent (1658 - 8 Nov 1727), Antoine Coypel (1661-1722), Jean-Marc Nattier (17 March 1685 - 7 November 1766), Nicolaas Verkolje (11 April 1673 - 21 January 1746). Francois Morellon la Cave (15 April 1696 - 9 July 1768; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 39 lines) - the pupil who carried Picart's book-illustration trade into English history publishing. Huguenot engraver, settled The Hague; married Jeanne Bouffet 1722; engraving of Vivaldi 1725; illustrated Voltaire's Henriade and Tragedies, Garcilaso de la Vega's Comentarios Reales (Paris ed. 1731), Rapin de Thoyras's History of England vols I-X (1724-27); engravings after Hogarth and Coypel; portraits of Edward Pococke, Nader Shah, Prince William Duke of Cumberland, Willem de Fesch; active London c.1731-34 and c.1750; self-portrait 1765. He is main 1 rather than top pick because he is the pupil who left the workshop for London, not the collaborator who stayed in it: the trade carried outward, where Houbraken is the line carried forward. The doorway is the pupil who engraved Vivaldi and then illustrated the history of England. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#543 as a candidate (appears only as Picart's pupil in the #544 top-pick justification). Gerard Hoet (22 August 1648 - 2 December 1733; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 185 lines) - the painter-engraver who designed the Bible Picart's shop engraved. 119 of the 214 picture-bible designs are his -- more than Picart's own 64; founded a drawing academy at Utrecht 1697 with Hendrick Schoock; published a book on drawing 1712 (103 prints by Pieter Bodart); source for Houbraken's Bentvueghels list; art dealer with Jacques Ignatius de Roore; trained by his father Moses Hoet and Warnard van Rijsen. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the designer behind the engraving, not the engraver: the man who drew the Bible and then taught a city to draw, where Houbraken is the man who engraved the faces. The doorway is the man who drew the Bible and then taught a city to draw. Etienne Picart le Romain (1632-1721; dates from search snippets only, not from a page read end to end) - the father, and the root of the line. Graveur du roi; Rome 1655-61 with Guillaume Vallet; pupil of Gilles Rousselet (not Francois de Poilly, per the British Museum); Academie Royale member 1664; last years from 1710 in the Netherlands, joining his son in Amsterdam; Bernard's first teacher. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the root before the trunk, not the continuation after it: the king's engraver whose son taught Amsterdam to engrave, where Houbraken is the son who carried the Amsterdam line on. The doorway is the king's engraver whose son taught Amsterdam to engrave. Prosper Marchand (11 March 1678 - 14 June 1756; dates from search snippets only, not from a page read end to end) - the bookseller-bibliographer who fled Paris with Picart and became the book-trade half of his world. Paris shop rue Saint-Jacques under the Phenix (with Gabriel II Martin); The Hague refuge with Picart Jan 1710; abandoned bookselling c.1713; proofreader for Fritsch & Bohm, Rotterdam; editor Journal litteraire 1713-1737; Dictionnaire historique (1758-59, posthumous); London 1726-1735; left his papers to Leiden University Library; with Picart and pupil Jakob van der Schley arranged illustrations for numerous books, supplying descriptions and rough sketches. He is the reserve because he is the trade, not the hand: the man who sketched the pictures Picart engraved, but whose own drawn work is the description, not the plate. The doorway is the refugee bookseller who sketched the pictures Picart engraved. Johann Philipp Endelich (1700 - buried 18 Sept 1760; dates from search snippets only, not from a page read end to end) - the pupil who became a portraitist in pastel. Pupil of Bernard Picart (RKD, per the Picart article); portraitist; engravings after his own drawings; one striking pastel. He is alternate 1 because his record is thin and his dates rest on one source, but he is the only Picart pupil who worked in pastel, and the pastel is the one medium the engraving school never touched. The doorway is the pupil who drew his own face and then engraved it. He is alternate 2 because he is the traveller, not the engraver: the man who brought Persepolis to Amsterdam, where Picart only drew its front door. The doorway is the traveller who brought Persepolis to Amsterdam and had Picart draw its front door. (The 11 names carried in #543 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Jurriaan Andriessen (1742-1819), Hermanus Numan (1744-1820), Jacques-Philippe Le Bas (1707-1783), Jacob Ernst Marcus (1774-1826) (mains); Izaak Jansz de Wit (1744-1809) (reserve); Hendrik Kobell (1751-1779), Jacques Kuyper (1761-1808) (alternates); spares: Pieter Barbiers II (1749-1842), Jacob Haafner (1754-1809), Jacob Otten Husly (16 November 1738 - 11 January 1796), Jan Bulthuis (1750-1801). Jurriaan Andriessen (1742-1819; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Brabant companion who painted the walls of Amsterdam houses and taught the next engraver in the chain. Wall-covering and theatre-decoration painter; travelled to Brabant with Vinkeles and Izaak Schmidt in 1765; subject, with Schmidt and Vinkeles himself, of the 1765 triple portrait; teacher of Hermanus Numan at the Stadstekenacademie in 1771. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the travelling companion, not the trunk: the painter beside the engraver on the road, whose wall-coverings are the large-scale cousin of Vinkeles's theatre sets. The doorway is the Brabant companion who painted the walls of Amsterdam houses -- and taught the next engraver in the chain. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; the 20 "Andriessen" log mentions are all Louis Andriessen (1939-2021), the composer, studied in the Rzewski chain -- never Jurriaan; no local file; not carried in #522-#543 as a subject. Dates search-verified only (Alamy/WGA captions). Hermanus Numan (1744-1820; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Paris meeting who became the copyist, theorist and publisher of the Amsterdam engraving line. Engraver, copyist, theorist and publisher; met Vinkeles in Paris in 1770; travelled to Paris in 1768, studied engraving there, then studied under Andriessen at the Stadstekenacademie in 1771; later Felix Meritis like Vinkeles and Vrijdag. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the theorist of the line, not its trunk: the man who wrote about engraving as well as engraving, and whose career runs parallel to Vinkeles's from the same Paris meeting. The doorway is the Paris meeting who became the copyist, theorist and publisher of the Amsterdam engraving line. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#543 as a subject. Jacques-Philippe Le Bas (1707-1783; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Paris master whose dry needle Vinkeles crossed Europe to learn, and whose workshop was the largest in Paris. French engraver; head of the largest engraving workshop in Paris; teacher of Cochin, Cathelin, Strange and Ryland, as well as of Vinkeles for a year in 1770-71; Vinkeles lived in his house and learned his dry-needle technique there. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the French master behind the Dutch pupil: the technique itself, not the Amsterdam line -- and a study of him is a study of Paris, not of the chain that runs through Punt. The doorway is the Paris master whose dry needle Vinkeles crossed Europe to learn -- and whose workshop was the largest in Paris. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#543 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia Jacques-Philippe Le Bas article, search-verified only (not read end to end in this run). Jacob Ernst Marcus (1774-1826; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the pupil who took Vinkeles's late, loose manner and turned it into a school of his own. Engraver; "one of the few pupils Vinkeles accepted"; also trained in drawing by Steven Goble; gold medal at the Stadstekenacademie 1798; later professor/director of the Akademie van Beeldende Kunsten (1820); founder of the society Kunst Zij Ons Doel with Harmanus Fock and Harmanus Vinkeles; his 106-print Studiebeelden en Fragmenten (1807-16) is the next generation's drawing book; his 1812 etchings portray Jacobus Buys, Izaak Schmidt and Jurriaan Andriessen -- the Vinkeles circle, one generation on. He is main 5 rather than top pick because he is the continuation after Vinkeles, not the trunk before him: the pupil whose handling derives from Vinkeles's late manner, and the proof the school outlived the master. The doorway is the pupil who took Vinkeles's late, loose manner and turned it into a school of his own. Painter; met Vinkeles in Paris in 1770. He is the reserve because he is a near-name collision with Jacob de Wit (study #541) and almost nothing is recorded beyond the Paris meeting -- but the meeting itself is dated and the confusion is worth one study to clear. The doorway is the namesake met in Paris -- a different de Wit from the master of the ceilings. He is alternate 1 because his life is short and his oeuvre small, but his Pax membership and his marine etchings put him at the centre of the circle Vinkeles drank in. The doorway is the marine etcher who died at twenty-eight -- a fellow of the Pax circle. (The 13 names carried in #542 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Cornelis Ploos van Amstel (4 January 1726 - 20 December 1798) (mains); Louis Fabritius Dubourg (bapt. 5 July 1693 - buried 22 September 1775) (reserve); Simon Fokke (1712-1784), Jakob van der Schley (26 July 1715 - 7 February 1779) (alternates); spares: Jan Caspar Philips (1690-1775), Pieter Barbiers I (bapt. 23 April 1717 - 7 September 1780), Jan Baptist Xavery (Antwerp 1697 - 1742, The Hague), Gerard Joseph Xavery (27 July 1700 - after 1747), Jacob Folkema (1692-1767), Jan de Beijer (24 September 1703 - c.1780), Jacob van Huysum (bapt. 25 February 1688 - 1740), Caspar Jacobsz Philips (1732-1789). Cornelis Ploos van Amstel (4 January 1726 - 20 December 1798) - the lumber merchant who married Troost's daughter, collected five thousand drawings, and invented a way to print them a century before photography. Pupil of Norbert van Bloemen and of Jacobus Buys (carried #541 main); married Elisabeth Troost, daughter of Cornelis Troost (carried #541 main); a lumber merchant by trade and a collector of 5,000+ drawings including Rembrandts. He invented the prenttekening -- a transfer/offset method of reproducing drawings in the crayon manner, from 1765; his Hoofdwerk ran to 46 prints at 350 impressions each. His collection sold in 1800 for 109,406 guilders. His pupils included Christina Chalon, William V Prince of Orange, Elisabeth van Woensel and Hermanus Petrus Schouten; he reported Petrus Camper's facial-angle findings; a member of Arte et Amicitia. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the collector's answer to the engraver's question: not the man who made the prints, but the man who figured out how to print the drawings themselves, and the proof that the print trade had a second half Punt never touched. The doorway is the lumber merchant who married Troost's daughter, collected five thousand drawings, and invented a way to print them a century before photography. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#542 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. He appears in the #541 file only as network/provenance (leg-2 note, "ex Cornelis Ploos van Amstel collection"; patrons entry 27), never as a candidate. FLAG: one source gives "Print made by: Cornelis Ploos van Amstel in 1559-1760" -- an obvious typo for 1759-1760. Louis Fabritius Dubourg (bapt. 5 July 1693 - buried 22 September 1775) - the history painter who swapped the brush for the burin and taught the academy circle to etch. History painter who became an engraver; pupil of Gerard de Lairesse (carried #529 spare), Gerrit Rademaker (1672-1711) and Jacob van Huysum (proposed spare 7); sexton of the Amstelkerk from 1718; cooperated with Picart in 1729; made the title plate for van Gool's Nieuw Schouburg (1750); a central figure of the Stadstekenacademie circle (Folkema, Morellon, Troost, Fokke, Elgersma, Tanje); works at the Westerkerk, Nieuwe Kerk and Town Hall; "exchanged brush for graver"; his collection was auctioned in 1776; Ploos ranked him with Isaac de Moucheron, de Wit and Troost. He is the reserve because he is the hinge between the painter's trade and the engraver's: the man who made the same crossing Punt made in reverse (Punt went from needle to brush late; Dubourg went from brush to graver), but his own engraved oeuvre is thin and his dates rest on one article. The doorway is the history painter who swapped the brush for the burin and taught the academy circle to etch. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#542 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Simon Fokke (1712-1784) - the booksellers' engraver who put the whole Dutch history into small plates. Pupil of J. C. Philips (proposed spare 1); the booksellers' engraver of small portraits and vignettes; plates for Wagenaar's Vaderlandsche Historie 1749-59; Danish portraits for Tycho Hofman (1741); after Vernet, Avercamp, Terborch, Ribera and Troost (a burlesque Death of Dido); the title vignette for Rousseau's Discourse on Inequality (1755); his portrait was painted by John Greenwood after Jacobus Buys. The doorway is the booksellers' engraver who put the whole Dutch history into small plates. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#542 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Jakob van der Schley (26 July 1715 - 7 February 1779) - the boy who finished his dead master's plates and then mapped the world for the book trade. Pupil of Picart from about age 12; completed unfinished plates after Picart's death; made the memorial portrait of Picart (1734); maps for Prevost's Histoire generale des voyages (publisher Pieter de Hondt, The Hague); illustrations for La Vie de Marianne (1735-47); Brantome frontispieces (1740); L'Imprimerie (1739); taught Philippus van der Schley (1724-1817); cooperated with Prosper Marchand. The doorway is the boy who finished his dead master's plates and then mapped the world for the book trade. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#542 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Painter and engraver; studied under Cornelis Pronk, Jacob de Wit and Cornelis Troost; director of the Amsterdam Drawing Academy; joined the Guild of St Luke in 1750; bas-reliefs, tapestry designs, book illustrations and theatre scenes. (The 14 names carried in #540 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt), Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem), Ludolf Bakhuizen (28 December 1630 or 1632, Emden - 7 November 1708, Amsterdam) (mains); Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695) (reserve); Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen), Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem) (alternates); spares: Aernout Smit (1641, Amsterdam - 1710), Michiel Maddersteg (bapt. 5 January 1662, Enkhuizen - d. 1708, Berlin), Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn), Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich), Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich), Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich), Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - after 1693), Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam). Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. German Baroque landscape and animal painter; family fled to Amsterdam in the Thirty Years' War (1640); trained with Guilliam du Gardijn, Cornelis de Bie and Barent Graat, but Berchem and Dujardin shaped him; worked for Ernst, Landgrave of Hesse-Rheinfels 1654-1659; court painter to Charles I Louis, Elector Palatine, 1664; moved to Frankfurt 1667; died in a house fire in 1685 trying to save his belongings. Specialised in pastoral idylls - idealized landscapes with ancient ruins, shepherds, sheep and cattle, the longing for harmony between men and animals and nature; never went to Italy, built his ruins from engravings; also one of the finest German portraitists of his time; many individual animal drawings (Albertina), intensifying the individuality of each species; Goethe called him the "Raphael of all livestock painters"; sons Philipp Peter Roos (Rosa di Tivoli, 1655-1706) and Johann Melchior Roos (1663-1731) were also painters. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the German pastoral before Kobell, the model Govert van der Leeuw painted "in the manner of" and one of the masters Kobell engraved after - the animal piece as an ideal, not a copy. The doorway is the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions as a study subject; no local file; not carried in #522-#539 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. Dutch painter and art dealer; winter landscapes, cityscapes, harbour scenes, beach views, river views, village scenes; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1664; may have been a pupil of Caesar van Everdingen (no influence of Everdingen visible - Heeremans was purely a landscape painter, not a figure painter); style shaped by Klaes Molenaer, but brighter in palette and livelier in movement, villagers skating, sledging, fishing and talking; frequently returned to Scheveningen beach and Egmond village, beach and ruins; pictures often go in pairs; collaborated with specialist figure painters - Abraham Storck painted the figures in Winter landscape with the Montelbaanstoren, Amsterdam; signed "THMANS" with the letters run together, so dealers long misread him as "Frederik Hendrik Mans". He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the staffage problem from the other side: a landscapist who hired the figures in, the mirror of van de Velde the hired staffage hand. The doorway is the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans.
