(The 13 names carried in #531 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Cornelis Bloemaert II (1603 - 28 September 1692; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - second son, painter turned engraver; studied with his father, his brothers Hendrick and Adriaen, and Gerard van Honthorst; learned printmaking from Crispijn van de Passe the Elder. Paris 1630 (engravings for Michel de Marolles's Temple des Muses), Rome from 1633 until his death - 59 years, most of his career. Bentvueghels bent-name "Winter". His Roman workshop attracted young engravers from abroad and spread Italian art through Europe; best-known engravings after Annibale Carracci (The Holy Family), Pietro da Cortona (Adoration of the Shepherds) and Rubens (Meleager); 1664-77 he worked on the engraved record of Cortona's Palazzo Pitti frescoes. Pupils Michel Natalis and Gilles Rousselet. He delayed returning to see his dying father so long that his father died - he stayed in Rome. The doorway is the son who went: the father never went to Italy and learned Caravaggism second-hand - now follow the son who did go, stayed 59 years, and engraved Italy for the rest of Europe. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Death date flagged as disputed: Wikipedia gives 28 September 1692; the NGA gives "in or after 1684" and Te Papa "c. 1684"; an older cyclopedia says 1680. 1692 is the commonly cited date. Leonaert Bramer (24 December 1596 - buried 10 February 1674; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Delft painter of genre, religious and history scenes, listed by the RKD among Bloemaert's pupils. At 18 he left on a long trip reaching Rome in 1616 via Arras, Amiens, Paris, Aix, Marseille, Genoa and Livorno; in Rome he was a founder of the Bentvueghels (bent-name "Nestelg...") and lived with Wouter Crabeth, once got into a fight with Claude Lorrain, and acquired the sobriquet "Leonardo della Notte" - Leonardo of the Night - for his nocturnes. He met Wybrand de Geest in Aix-en-Provence in 1616. Back in Delft by 1628, Guild of St. Luke 1629; painted frescoes, a rarity north of the Alps (not survived), and murals on canvas. A lifelong bachelor, productive until his death at 77; it is possible, though undocumented, that Vermeer trained with him. One of the most intriguing personalities in 17th-century Dutch art. The doorway is the Delft pupil who ran off to Rome at eighteen, helped found the Bentvueghels, fought with Claude Lorrain, and came home to paint the night. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Jan Baptist Weenix (1621-1659; Leiden Collection gives 1660/61 - death date disputed, flag it; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - painter of the Dutch Golden Age, Bloemaert pupil (after Jan Micker, before Claes Moeyaert). Born near Amsterdam harbour, son of an architect; travelled to Italy 1643-47, joined the Bentvueghels as "Ratel" (rattle, for a speech defect), worked for Cardinal Camillo Pamphili (later Pope Innocent X), painted a portrait of Rene Descartes, and in 1649 became master of the Utrecht Guild of St. Luke alongside Jan Both as officer. Mainly responsible for introducing the Italian harbour scene into Dutch art - mid-size paintings with a group of figures in the foreground - alongside Italian landscapes with large figures among ruins, seaside views, and late still lifes of dead game and dogs. With Berchem and Jan Both, a leader of the second generation of Dutch Italianate painters; teacher of his son Jan Weenix and nephew Melchior d'Hondecoeter. Died at 39 at Huis ter Mey, Vleuten. The doorway is the Utrecht pupil who took the master's Italianate landscape and turned it into the Dutch harbour scene - and died at thirty-nine, leaving a son who became the great still-life painter. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. (Mains 2, 3 and 4; main 1 is the top pick above.) Reserve: Wybrand Simonsz. de Geest (16 August 1592 - c. 1661; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Frisian portrait painter, the most important portraitist of Friesland. Learned first from his father Simon Juckesz, a stained-glass worker, then studied with Bloemaert in Utrecht. Grand Tour 1614-18 through France and Italy; met Leonaert Bramer in Aix-en-Provence in 1616; in Rome joined the Bentvueghels as "De Friesche Adelaar" (the Frisian Eagle) and kept an album amicorum (friendship book) from 1611, 22 drawings from which are still missing. Returned to Leeuwarden c. 1620 and became the leading portrait painter of the Frisian nobility and the Orange-Nassau family; married Hendrickje Fransdr Uylenburgh in 1622, niece of Saskia van Uylenburgh - Rembrandt's wife - linking him by marriage to Rembrandt's circle; influenced Jacob Adriaensz Backer; students Jan Jansz. de Stomme and Jacob Potma; sons Julius and Frank also became painters. The doorway is the Frisian pupil who carried Utrecht portraiture north to Leeuwarden, married into Rembrandt's family, and signed his Roman years "the Frisian Eagle". VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Alternates if any of the 5 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Jacob Gerritsz. Cuyp (1594-1651/52; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dordrecht portrait and landscape painter, RKD-listed pupil of Bloemaert; son of stained-glass designer Gerrit Gerritsz. Cuyp, half-brother of Benjamin Gerritsz. Cuyp, father of the far more famous Aelbert Cuyp. Joined the Dordrecht Guild of St. Luke 18 July 1617, treasurer 1629/33/37/41; helped found the Dordrecht painters' breakaway guild in 1642 and is called the nestor of the Dordrecht painters. Best known today for portraits - especially fine portraits of children - and pastoral landscapes; early work shows distinct Caravaggesque influence from his Utrecht training. Teacher of Ferdinand Bol, Aelbert Cuyp, Benjamin Cuyp, Paulus Lesire, Aert van der Neer and Pieter Hermansz Verelst; Jacob's recommendation is said to have sent Nicolaes Maes and Ferdinand Bol to Rembrandt. The doorway is the Utrecht pupil who went home to Dordrecht, founded its painting school, and taught the father of the golden light - Aelbert Cuyp's father, and Rembrandt's Bol. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Birth year flagged: 1594 is the current consensus against 1575 in the 9th edition of the Britannica - the old date is wrong, use 1594. Nicolaus Knupfer (1609 - 15 October 1655; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch Golden Age painter, born Leipzig, apprenticed there to Emanuel Nysen, then Magdeburg making brushes for artists until 1630, when he moved to Utrecht to work with Bloemaert - living with him for two years - before setting up his own Utrecht studio (visiting member of the Guild of St. Luke, 1637). Painted small-scale biblical, literary and mythological scenes and popular genre works; worked on the decorations of Kronborg Castle in Denmark; painted the figures in the landscapes of Jan Both and Jan Baptist Weenix - the staffage hand inside two other Bloemaert pupils' pictures. A successful teacher: his students included Jan Steen, Gabriel Metsu, Ary de Vois and Pieter Crijnse Volmarijn. The doorway is the Leipzig brush-maker who lived two years in the master's house, then painted the little figures inside Jan Both's and Weenix's landscapes - and taught Jan Steen. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Spares (verified, NOT part of the 7): Hieronymus Francken I (c. 1540-1610; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Flemish painter, pupil of Frans Floris, worked at Fontainebleau 1566-72, court painter to the French court from 1594; Bloemaert's Paris/Fontainebleau master 1581-83. Elegant Mannerist groups of dancers, musicians and courtiers, uniting Antwerp, Venetian and French elements. Brother of Frans Francken I and Ambrosius Francken I; father of a painting dynasty. The doorway is the Fontainebleau master who gave the boy from Gorinchem the only real training he ever got. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Adriaen Bloemaert (after 1609 - buried 8 January 1666; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - fourth son, first instruction from his father; Italy until 1637, then Vienna, Salzburg (employed there), Freistadt 1640, back in Utrecht by 1651; painted history and portraits "with some success". THIN RECORD WARNING: few works securely attributed; most biographical detail comes from Houbraken/Bryan via the RKD. The doorway is the youngest-but-one son who took the family trade to Vienna and Salzburg and came home to die in Utrecht. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Frederick Bloemaert (c. 1614/17-1690; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - youngest artist son, engraver; published his father's Artes Apellae liber (1650) - the drawing book that taught Utrecht to draw into the 19th century. Dates as commonly cited (c. 1614/17) - flag as approximate. THIN RECORD WARNING: known chiefly as his father's publisher/engraver rather than for independent work. The doorway is the son who turned his father's drawings into the textbook every Utrecht artist learned from. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Andries Both (1612/13 - 23 March 1642; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - elder brother of Jan Both, Bloemaert pupil; Rouen 1633, Rome from 1635, shared a studio with Jan van Causteren, then lived with his brother on the Via Vittoria from 1638; a leading bambocciante - low-life Roman genre scenes after Pieter van Laer, influenced by Brueghel and Adriaen Brouwer - while his brother painted idyllic landscapes. Drowned in a Venetian canal in 1642, aged 29 or 30, on the way home. The doorway is the elder Both brother, who painted Rome's low life while his brother painted its golden light - and drowned on the way home. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Cornelis de Beer (dates unknown) - RKD-listed Bloemaert pupil; son of Joos de Beer (Bloemaert's own second teacher) - the teacher's son becoming the pupil's pupil. THIN RECORD WARNING: no dates or secure works surfaced in this leg's searches; propose only if a deeper dig finds material. The doorway is the son of Bloemaert's own teacher, who came back to learn from the boy his father once taught. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Gerrit Splinter (dates unknown) and Joos de Beer (active 1575-91) - Bloemaert's first two Utrecht teachers - Splinter the drunken dauber who lasted two weeks, de Beer "not the best of painters". THIN RECORD WARNING: both are known almost entirely through van Mander's anecdotes; no independent oeuvre to study. List as a pair only; either alone is too thin for a full study. The doorway is the two bad masters - the drunk and the mediocrity - whose failure sent a fifteen-year-old to Paris.
